#6885. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Microsoft has launched Frontier Company, making a $2.5 billion bet that the next competitive battleground in artificial intelligence will not be foundation models, but helping enterprises put those models to work.

#6883. Please Just Tell Me Who the Next James Bond Is Already

Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor are reportedly all up for the role. But I’m not having fun anymore.

Some movie announcements arrive as a complete surprise. Ryan Gosling as Ghost Rider? Very cool idea. Nathan Fielder secretly spending weeks staring into Elizabeth Holmes’s eyes for You Can See Everything? Amazing. In other instances, film studios will delay an announcement everyone knows is coming until their audience finds itself sucked up into the maelstrom and spun around until they’re so dizzy that they’re puking up that morning’s Cheerios. Maybe they wish they never even got excited in the first place.

That’s where I’m at with the current race for James Bond, which has reportedly come down to three actors: Jack Lowden (Slow Horses), Callum Turner (One Night Only), and Josh O’Connor (Disclosure Day). The three finalists were announced via Puck three weeks ago on October 22, along with a mysterious fourth actor whose identity has not yet been revealed, if he even exists.

Since then, there have been no official updates from Amazon/MGM—which acquired full control of the franchise in February 2025—but there have been dozens upon dozens of interviews from former Bond actors as well as anyone remotely associated with the prior 25 Bond films and novels, with each interviewee expressing that all three men up for the role would probably be great.

“All the men who are on the list now are actors I admire,” Pierce Brosnan recently told Vanity Fair. Maybe true but after a year of this guessing game, it just isn’t fun anymore. Even worse, no one seems concerned with even making it fun.

“It's an incredibly bizarre pinch-me moment... even being uttered in that same sentence and being considered for something like that,” Josh O’Connor told BBC News on Tuesday. “But I am afraid, there is nothing to say beyond that.”

Callum Turner was even more direct: “I’m not going to comment on it just because there’s no point in commenting on it in this moment,” he told Esquire UK in August.

Then, Lowden uttered “Oh, Christ” in an interview earlier this week when he finally addressed the rumors. “It’s all very flattering. It’s nice to be mentioned—for any role—and I’m just not really going to add to the noise.”

Are they not allowed to even say they want the role? Am I supposed to act excited when they finally announce if they don’t even seem excited themselves? Do we even want to see a film with Turner, Lowden, or O’Connor as James Bond?

Please add to the noise, guys. I’m begging you. It’s time to tell us.

#6882. Colin Jost Takes Gift-Giving Seriously

The Weekend Update co-host and Kay campaign star shares his approach to holiday shopping.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Finding the perfect holiday gift isn’t easy. Between the pressure to get it right, the endless wish lists, and the people who insist they don’t want anything, shopping for loved ones can feel like an impossible mission. Even Colin Jost, who has spent years navigating the pressures of live television, admits that the process is incredibly stressful.

This festive season, the Weekend Update co-host is putting his comedic talents to work with Kay by starring in the jeweler’s latest holiday campaign, “Love All In.” The cheeky ad follows Jost on an increasingly ridiculous quest to find the perfect present, poking fun at the lengths we’ll go to for the people closest to us. It’s a fitting partnership for someone who knows a thing or two about making people laugh but also takes gifting pretty seriously.

“I think I’m a good gift giver,” Jost tells ELLE. “Giving gifts is stressful because I do want it to be a meaningful gift. I hate the idea of just buying something because you have to give someone something.”

For Jost, the secret to getting it right has less to do with how much you spend and more to do with how much thought goes into it. “At every kind of price point for any gift, there [are] thoughtful and unthoughtful versions,” he says. “Something can be really expensive but not thoughtful.”

It’s a sentiment that aligns with Kay’s latest campaign, which encourages shoppers to go “all in” when expressing their love. And while Jost might not be the first person you’d think to turn to for jewelry advice, he’s developed an appreciation for it over the years, thanks to his very chic wife, Scarlett Johansson.

“Until five years ago, I didn’t own a watch,” he admits. That changed when the actress gifted him his first one, introducing him to an entirely new world of accessories. As for whether she’s influenced his personal style? “In that it’s exclusively managed by her, yes, I would say she’s influenced it.”

Despite his Staten Island roots, Jost’s jewelry preferences are surprisingly understated. “I don’t really wear a ton of jewelry, but I also am from Staten Island, so I do have to have certain pieces, or else they won’t let me back over the Verrazano Bridge,” he jokes.

While browsing Kay’s assortment, he found himself drawn to simple gold pieces, including a ring, bracelet, and necklace. “They were really nice, but also they weren’t gaudy or anything,” he says. His preference for gold also comes with some rather unconventional reasoning: “If we get into a post-apocalyptic situation, it feels like it’s better to melt down for trading.”

When it comes to shopping for the people closest to him, Jost admits that both Johansson and his Weekend Update collaborator Michael Che are difficult to buy things for, though he found a creative solution for the latter. “I got Che a really large and heavy, realistic statue of a velociraptor,” he recalls. “It was so heavy that he literally can’t get rid of it.” The real expense wasn’t the dinosaur itself. “The main cost was shipping,” he says. “It was a huge eyesore, and it’s prohibitively expensive for him to have removed from his property.”

The hardest person to gift on Jost’s list? His father, who insists he doesn’t need anything. “I think he actually means it, but it’s so unhelpful,” Jost says. After years of nostalgic gifts inspired by his dad’s past, he’s running out of ideas. “I’ve got to figure out something that breaks through his defenses.”

In terms of gifting etiquette, Jost has a few rules of his own. For starters, he’s big on surprises. While he understands the appeal of a wish list for children, he thinks adults should leave a little room for the unexpected. “For adults, it’s got to be ‘surprise me,’” he says. “The surprise is half the fun, three-quarters of the fun.” Regifting, on the other hand, comes with a few conditions. He’s open to the idea, but only if the gift is a perfect fit for someone and there’s full disclosure.

But perhaps the most surprising revelation comes when I ask what’s on his own holiday wish list. After spending much of our conversation discussing what to buy everyone else, Jost seems relieved to finally be on the receiving end. “Oh, thank you! Finally, someone asked me. What do I want?”

His answer? Nothing material. “At this point in life, I mostly want experiences,” he says. Whether it’s a trip or simply someone taking the initiative to organize an outing, he’s more interested in making memories than collecting more things. “Even if it’s just the plan of, ‘Let’s go do this at this time,’ I think that’s a great gift.” Or, when in doubt, a velociraptor.

#6881. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Microsoft has launched Frontier Company, making a $2.5 billion bet that the next competitive battleground in artificial intelligence will not be foundation models, but helping enterprises put those models to work.

#6878. A Straight Razor Will Give You the Best Shave of Your Life. Here Are 6 Great Options to Buy.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

If you get razor bumps and ingrowns, don’t be afraid to go old-school.

We’re 120 years past the invention of the safety razor and 50 years past the invention of the cartridge razor. So yes, we understand how annoying it is to even suggest that you should shave with the thing that’s even older—technically as old as shaving itself. Go ahead, make fun of the old-school dandy men’s magazine saying you should be straight-razor shaving with a shaving cream that requires a boar hair brush and smells of tobacco. But to turn shaving from a chore to something you look forward to, well, the straight razor is a one-way ticket to hobby town.

Using an electric razor or beard trimmer is great for getting rid of scruff, but if you ever go for a clean shave, a straight razor is the best tool—full stop. They are the best for your face, don’t give you as many ingrown hairs, and (unlike the five-bladed cartridge razors) don’t get clogged up with hairs after two shaves. Plus they add a sense of elegance to your everyday routine, not to mention a bit of danger, à la Sweeney Todd. Only joking, of course.

So sure, challenge our claim that straight-razor shaving is as good as it gets. Or better yet, try it for a little bit and then tell us we’re wrong. If you clean shave and care about grooming, consider picking up a straight razor and doing it the old-fashioned way.

Frankly, most of these straight razors aren’t even technically straight razors. They are shavettes; hybrids with disposable blades so you don’t have to worry about honing and sharpening (and all the hazards that come with failing to do so). You still get the straight razor vibe, but it’s safer and more convenient. For a starter shavette, you can pick up the Parker SRX straight edge. It’s compatible universally with any double edge blades, so you won’t ever run out.

Take care of this stainless steel beauty and it will retain that sheen for years without a sign of rust. This all makes it a great, affordable pick for intermediate shavers who prioritize performance. For a more gateway razor, I’d pick the Dovo shavette

Fundamentally, this is the same thing as the previous Parker razor. It’s a shavette that uses double-edge disposable blades, so the experience of using it and the accompanying learning curve are the exact same. The difference is the wood handle. It looks great, but if you get anything other than shaving cream on there, it’s certainly not going to be as easy to clean as stainless steel. I still wouldn’t recommend a novice pick this up, but that’s what the safety razor is for.

If you are genuinely just curious about this whole thing, you don’t need to drop even $30 on something you may use once and then put down forever. You can pick up a Classic Samurai razor from Amazon for less than 15 bucks. It’s not the best ever, and you won’t be showing off to your friends, but it’s a way to get your feet wet. Plus it comes with enough extra (safety-razor-compatible) blades that if you do find the knack for straight-razor shaving, you can upgrade to a Parker or Dovo and have plenty of blades to use.

The straight handle is your less flexible, slightly sturdier alternative to your folding shavette. This Feather razor is Japanese made with a straight grip meant to give more fine tuning and control to the average shaver. I only wish that, like Parker shavettes, it was compatible with all double-blade replacements—not just the Feather branded ones.

Whether straight handles are actually easier to control and safer than folding ones is up for debate. Barbers have their preferences and if you are looking to start shaving with a straight razor at home, so will you. If you’ve tried the folding shavettes and can’t quite get a handle (sorry for the pun) on them, this one is worth a shot.

As we exit the shavette category, I turn once again to Dovo. If you want a razor that looks and feels as traditional as it gets while still being brand new, you need a Dovo.

This isn’t for beginners or really even intermediate shavers. If anything, it’s more of a novelty. If you want to use it for real, you should probably be a real barber. If you have the skills (and the disposable income), this will be something fun to whip out for your favorite clients in need of the cleanest shave of their life.

There’s something uniquely satisfying about buying vintage. These come from the era when these were the only razors men used to shave. In a way, that’s how they know they are quality. Just old-fashioned stainless steel blades and a design that modern “retro” can’t come close to replicating.

You can buy these (one per package despite the website’s imagery) sealed or opened and cleaned Friedrich Schlemper straight razors for less than the price of a Dovo. These Sistrum razors are German-made carbon steel, likely dating back to sometime between the 1950s and the 1970s.

Classic Straight Razor: Traditionally, a straight razor has one steel (typically stainless or carbon) blade. In the old days, before the safety and cartridge razors we know and love, this was the only option. Barbers had to hone and sharpen their blades using a leather strap—and if you go classic this is still a requirement. This makes a straight razor an investment that can go south if you don’t take care of it properly. Serious professionals only—it’s not easy to learn how to shave safely with one of these. There’s a reason that as a society, we don’t use them anymore.

Shavettes: This modern hybrid of the safety razor and straight razor is what I recommend over the classic style. They are much more affordable, for starters, and the replaceable blades are much safer, especially for at-home experimentation. If you are a casual looking into straight razors, I cannot stress enough to start and end with a shavette.

Folding vs Straight Handle: In terms of shavettes, it all comes down to preference. A straight handle can be preferable to a lighter, airy folding blade handle due to better ergonomics and greater control. But others find a folding handle to allow them better control and stability. It’s all about whichever you feel allows you more precision while leading to fewer errors.

Esquire has been helping men look (and be) at their best since the heyday of the straight razor. We lived through it. If 90 years of institutional knowledge isn’t going to convince you, nothing will.

#6877. Kate Moss Proves the Leather Midi Skirt Is Eternally Appealing

Moss has always got a new way to make a classic item feel fresh.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Kate Moss is era-defining—as synonymous with the look of Cool Britannia in the ’90s as she is with Indie Sleaze. And yet, when you take apart Moss’s wardrobe, it’s clear to see that the supermodel has always stuck to the classics. Case in point: her look to attend Tom Ford’s SS27 show during Paris Fashion Week on September 30.

The 52-year-old arrived in support of her friend and the brand’s creative director, Haider Ackermann, wearing a tried-and-tested formula of a button-down Oxford shirt and sleek and shiny leather pencil skirt. Her take was loaded with sex appeal and timeless allure in equal measure.

The brilliance of Moss’s look came in its application. She’s always been great at styling familiar pieces in inventive ways to bring them back into rotation. Here, she left her shirt open entirely and rolled up the sleeves, with the high waist of the skirt stepping in at just the right point. Her choice of a string bra kept things feeling more contemporary than the more obvious choice of something lacy, perhaps. Sunglasses for the nighttime added A-list appeal, while her green stone ring reminded us of her often bohemian style sensibility. The final touch of strappy sandals finished Moss’s look with the sky-high heels she often turns to.

The cut of the skirt was key, too. Its pencil shape added to that classic ideal of sex-appeal dressing, feeling typically true of Tom Ford’s design signature that Ackermann now does his own way. It’s a shape that never goes out of style, whether it’s worn like Moss or in contrast with a roomy wool jumper or a lacy camisole.

It’s the skirt of the season. How will you wear yours?

#6875. Statement Scarves Are Fall’s Defining Accessory

Puffed, twisted, and totally oversize: Scarves are taking over as new statements.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The behemoth sculptural statement scarf is here, and it’s never looked better. Puffed, twisted, oversize, and opulent, these finishing pieces eclipse the coat and become a one-and-done look for winter weather. These are not your usual blanket scarves or faux fur creations. The new statement scarf is padded and sculpted, often done in fabrics like luscious leather. These XL scarves were seen on the fall/winter 2026 runway at Loewe, Celine, and Toteme. Elsewhere, Givenchy showcased the power of a smaller leather scarf done in brilliant cobalt blue. The main message with all of these outfit-defining scarves is that they do all the talking and create a strong visual impact with even the most simple of coats. Black leather jackets, neutral wool coats, basic blazers, and even beige trenches get a new look with a dramatic scarf. It’s no surprise the look is trending now. After season upon season of quiet luxury, we’re now firmly in the era of the statement coat.

But for those who don’t want to fully commit to the look, we have the statement scarf, which gives instant drama with ease. If you’re in need of styling inspiration, follow the runways’ lead. Keep it tonal and choose a scarf in the same color palette as your coat or jacket, or go the opposite way and pick up a primary hue like scarlet red or bright yellow to add contrast to a more subdued-hued jacket.

There’s also something beautifully haphazard about these pieces: On the runway, many of them are intentionally styled so that they look windswept or as if they were in motion and got frozen mid-moment. For designers like Michael Rider at Celine, that was part of it. The label’s fall/winter 2026 show notes presented the collection as “intuition over strategy. Feeling it rather than planning it,” and “contradiction, imperfection, character.” Adding to this, “I love when messy, complex, layered inner lives come through underneath great clothes,” Rider expressed in the show notes. “Thinking about people with style who wear beautiful clothes in a personal way.”

On a more practical level, the statement scarf adds a little bit of pizzazz with warmth. It acts as wearable sculpture. Tied tightly around the neck, styled with an over-the-top pair of sunglasses, it brings the focus to the face and creates an iconic look that is also strangely practical.

We’re all so bundled under layers in these cooler months that accessorizing (and wearing jewelry) can pose a challenge. Many of the big puffy scarves are editorial showpieces that may not have made it to the retail side of fashion, but the other side of statement scarves lies in embellishment. Think: embroidered wonders from Dries Van Noten, paillette triangle scarves from Zankov, or even just a classic scarf done with extra-long fringe. Just think of it as a warm version of a necklace.

#6873. Jennifer Lawrence Treats NYC to Another Dose of Primary Colors

And she paired her bright look with an IYKYK Hunger Games reference

Jennifer Lawrence is heading back to District 12. After confirming she will be reprising her role of Katniss Everdeen for the forthcoming Hunger Games prequel, Sunrise on the Reaping, the Oscar-winning actor stepped out in New York City in a look riddled with references to her alter-ego.

On November 3, Lawrence leaned into primary colors with a cheerful dose of blues and reds. Photographed on a walk, she was seen wearing an oversize blue barn jacket with a chocolate-brown corduroy collar over a white graphic crewneck tee with a bright drawing of Sesame Street’s Ernie. She contrasted the sky hues with a pair of baggy red trousers from Free City, and brought both the shades together with a plaid flannel tied around her waist. She slipped into some black Vans and headed out, flashing the Hunger Games three-finger salute to paparazzi for an IYKYK nod.

She also revived her character’s signature braid—a style she previously told Harper’s Bazaar she would never revisit: “I can’t put my hair in a braid without being like, ‘Oh, you want somebody to bring up the movie?’” (The long singular plait, braided to one side, was no doubt accomplished with a little help from her new haircare brand, Mes.)

Out on November 20, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping is set 24 years before Katniss volunteered as tribute for her sister. It follows a young Haymitch Abernathy (Joseph Zada) during the 50th Hunger Games, with Mckenna Grace as District 12 tribute Maysilee Donner, Elle Fanning as a young Effie Trinket, and Whitney Peak as Lenore Dove Baird. Lawrence, along with Josh Hutcherson (as Peeta Mellark) and Woody Harrelson (as an older Haymitch), have been confirmed to make appearances, though we do not yet know in what capacity.

#6872. Why Did Barry Keoghan Get XERF?

Well, the answer is very simple, according to his facialist.

In the past week, everyone seems to have discovered XERF. A radiofrequency treatment originally from Korea, it can tighten, tone, and lift skin, and it’s now becoming a verb. Kim Kardashian got XERFed. So did Priyanka Chopra, Martha Stewart, Cardi B, and also Barry Keoghan. Out of all the celebrities who have gotten it, it’s Keoghan who has made the most headlines.

His longtime facialist, Nilam Holmes, posted a video of Keoghan receiving XERF while he says in a voice-over that “It’s very relaxing and therapeutic. I feel better about myself; I look better. I think it’s important to look after yourself inside and outside.” It’s so unusual to see an actor, let alone a male actor, star in a beauty video that ELLE called up Holmes, a U.K.-based facialist whose clients include Rosie Huntington-Whiteley (whom she prepped for the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show) and Joe Alwyn, to learn more about red carpet protocols, how Keoghan got XERFed, and what she thinks of all the press attention.

XERF only just launched in the U.K. nine weeks ago, and there was a waiting list. Everyone’s been waiting so long to have it. Celebrities are sharing their experiences, which doesn’t usually happen with a lot of treatments. People have also been traveling to Korea to have it done and then going back to the U.S. to talk about it.

I invest in a lot of the top-of-the-line devices and use really reputable machines. Currently, I have things like Ultraformer III, Ultherapy, and Morpheus8. Most of them have some downtime, or they’re really painful—you’re breaking the skin and using heat. People can look like they have grid marks or are red for a bit.

XERF has no downtime and is not painful, so you don’t need numbing cream. I wouldn’t say it’s pain-free, but it’s not painful. I could do one now and somebody could go out tonight and be fine. You’re getting a big result quickly that carries on working, and it’s an alternative to those other treatments. It is probably one of the pricier things that I offer in my clinic. It’s a bit misleading when they say nonsurgical facelift. It’s more of a skin-tightening treatment.

When I did it on myself, I did half of my face, and I could see a considerable difference. One of the first XERF treatments that I did on a celebrity was on Nicole Scherzinger, and midway through, I ran out of the special gas that goes in the cooler and half of her face looked so different. It does give quite a dramatic, instant result.

People who don’t need a lot of lift get the best results. You see contours better. It gives a bit of a Cinderella effect, so the instant result looks better than it will look three days later. About 50 percent of people have come back and said it was great; they want a second session, and then others are like, “Yeah, it’s good, but I still think Morpheus was better.” You notice the most difference after one treatment. It’s quite a pricey treatment. I’ve not had anybody say they don’t notice a difference.

I do a lot of celebrities. The sort of higher up you go, the less you can mention. But what happened with Barry? Bless him. I did a lady here in the U.K. who is a big celebrity and normally every time she does something, the press picks it up, but this time, they didn’t; they picked Barry’s over hers. The press was very random. He does come in regularly. We don’t do anything crazy. He had his first XERF and was having his second one.

On the day he was coming in, I was meant to be filming my own face. He came really early and I was running really late and had said, “Barry, could you come back later? I’ve got to film myself having it.” He said, “I’ll do it for you,” and that was it. It wasn’t a setup. It wasn’t an endorsement. He just did it for me. The press was a little bit unfair—they said he was having it because trolls claimed he was rushing to have a treatment to change his look. Barry likes his looks. His commentary wasn’t, I look terrible. I don’t feel confident to go out. I don’t think that was the case at all.

XERF doesn’t have the ability to change anybody’s face, and a lot of the other treatments don’t either. Surgery or excessive fillers are the only things that change people’s faces. But he’s filming a movie and doing long hours. He does get a tired look on his skin. He likes to just do some things that give a little bit of a pick-me-up, and XERF does that. For poor Barry, people were saying, “I used to like you, and now I think you’re weak because you’re having something [done].” Some of the comments are like that. Well, it’s only a skin treatment.

Some old pictures from earlier this year were about his skin texture. We’ve done a little bit of micro-needling, and he has Hydrafacials, which have helped his texture a lot. It also helps when you’re lying down under good lighting as well. I’ve been looking after Barry for a while. We did him for Peaky Blinders. His skin looked great in that, but at the minute, he’s on a movie where they use prosthetic makeup and that doesn’t help the skin at all.

It’s not unusual for the guys to come in with bits of glue left from prosthetics and then that peels the skin a little bit as well. I’ve seen Barry since, and he’s fine. His agents were more unhappy about him doing that. He wasn’t saying that people should go and have those treatments.

But why shouldn’t men do skin treatments? At the moment, I have a higher percentage of male clients in my own clinic. And it’s not just actors, footballers, or pop stars. I have barristers, doctors, and high-flying professionals. [For actors], makeup ruins the skin, and they start looking more and more tired on camera. Most people do have some sort of facial when they’re on a movie and definitely before a red carpet. Now it’s the norm for everybody to have a facial prior to grooming.

I’m in London, but it’s got a very discreet and private entrance. Men realize when they come in that it’s actually a really nice experience. Men like the machines—the radiofrequency, the Hydrafacial—and they’re not into the fluffy lymphatic drainage massage. They want the tech. Once they see the difference, they’re very loyal. They don’t want to go and try somewhere else.

I don’t think it’s about that everyone’s trying to look younger. People are just trying to stay as they are. The longer you stay looking like that, the more parts you’re going to get. I’ve got actors in their eighties that still come.

Men are shamed into thinking it’s vanity or almost like, you’re defeated if you had something done. I don’t think that’s a nice message because we all want to look nice. We want to be comfortable with what we see in the mirror. I try to encourage that and take the shame out of it because it shouldn’t exist. What’s wrong with having a facial, a little collagen-boosting treatment, or micro-needling, which is one of the oldest, most organic ones you can have?

My attitude is that the firmer and healthier the skin, the more glowy it looks. Your skin doesn’t have to be wrinkle-free, but when you’ve got a bit of bounce and radiance, that makes you look younger. It doesn’t have to be stretched tight. It’s not preventing aging, but just slowing it down.

For two days, I would do a radiofrequency skin-tightening treatment—maybe not more than a Collagenwave or Exion treatment, because it will give you firmness and contour the face. Add a Hydrafacial to clean up the skin. Then, I would infuse some exosomes or a skin booster via a tiny micro-needling stamp. It basically gets into the surface of the skin. I put them under a red light. I just do some Dermalux light afterwards. If somebody wants to do something extra, EmFace is always good to add in because that helps to tone the muscles as well.

You could do it. Kim Kardashian did say, “This is my red carpet treatment.” Firstly, I think it’s too long a treatment to do for that. It’s also very expensive, too. But if I was getting someone ready for the Oscars, our prep would start a month before. Then, I would do something like XERF or micro-needling. I micro-needle with hyper-diluted Sculptra or exosomes. That takes about a month to get a full result. And I would also do dermaplaning, which takes all the peach fuzz away. When you put your makeup on, it reflects, rather than the hair absorbing the light.

I love a good eyebrow—we do Korean lamination. It softens the hairs of the brow or the lashes. It makes them more polished-looking, so that they fit really nicely and you can brush them up or across, making them look fuller so you can get a better shape out of them. I could do a skin peel in 10 minutes for somebody if they don’t have time, and then they can go straight into glam or grooming.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6869. We Found the Ultimate Cold-Weather Cologne

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fulton & Roark's new scent, inspired by a church in Gary, Indiana, is phenomenal.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

Halloween is in the rearview, Daylight Saving Time is over, and the sun has decided it has better places to be (although I am grateful for that extra hour of sleep we got). You know what all that means: It’s officially time to bring out the poofy coats, the cable-knit sweaters, and the winter scents. We’re talking spicy, smoky, sophisticated, and a little dark. Think of it as the olfactory equivalent of a cashmere scarf or quilted jacket. But before you reach for the same old colognes of winters past, consider making room for something new in your fragrance rotation.

Allow me to suggest my new cold-weather go-to, released just last month by one of my all-time favorite niche fragrance houses, Fulton & Roark. This extrait has a pretty sick name: Cathedral of Ruins—and it smells every bit as atmospheric as you’d hope for with a name like that. It’s moody without being gloomy, warm without being cloying, and oh-so intriguing. Put a spritz of this stuff on and you’re guaranteed to start hearing the words, What are you wearing?

Fulton & Roark does what it calls “American fine fragrance,” meaning it doesn’t follow all the rules of your classic European perfumery. The perfumer looks to new ingredients, formulation methods, and scent combinations for all of its fragrances. Oh, and every fragrance is unisex, truly unisex. My boyfriend loves the scents as much as I do, and we often share them.

And since this is American fine fragrance we’re talking about, it only makes sense that each of the brand’s fragrances are named after and inspired by a real American place with a story behind it. That rule even holds for the brand’s name: Fulton is for Fulton County, Georgia; and Roark is for Roark’s Cove in Sewanee, Tennessee. So where is this Cathedral of Ruins? Gary, Indiana. Birthplace of the Jackson 5, and our clearest example of Rust Belt deindustrialization. The once-booming steel city is now also home to thousands of abandoned and decaying buildings, including the City Methodist Church—once one of the grandest churches in the Midwest.

Fulton and Roark co-founder Kevin Keller showed me photos of what City Methodist Church looks like now, which he took on his own trip to Gary, and the immediate sense I got from these photos was a devastating sort of beauty (or a beautiful sort of devastation). The church’s roof has fallen in; the only living congregation is the vegetation that reclaims more and more of the space every year. But, its Neo-Gothic stone walls still stand. It’s a dead relic, but it is not a dead space. It pulls up mixed feelings. This Cathedral of Ruins extrait de parfum is Fulton & Roark’s homage to it, and it nails everything you get from seeing a picture of the place.

Like I said, Cathedral of Ruins is the perfect cold-weather scent. But instead of the typical rich, gourmand notes you get in fall and winter fragrances like vanilla, caramel, and amber, Cathedral of Ruins relies on spices, smoke, and woods to create a warm atmosphere. This is not a syrupy sweet winter scent—it’s smoldering, Gothic-inspired, sophisticated, and unlike any scent you’ve ever smelled before.

At the top, you get resinous cypress, spicy black peppercorn, and, unexpectedly, floral rose. The lush, velvety notes of rose lead into the more delicate floral notes of saffron, symbolizing the grace of the cathedral (and possibly the new natural growth of vegetation over the ruins). Rich Peru balsam finishes out the heart with more warm, woody vibes.

But the base is where this fragrance really stands out. The star of the show here is burned frankincense, the classic scent of sacred ritual—imagine smoke rising above the walls, past the ceiling that’s no longer there. Frankincense is, obviously, smoky, but not in the same way as something like tobacco; the frankincense here is dry, resinous, and subtly fresh like pine. To me, frankincense even has a waxy-green quality similar to olives. The warm depth of leathered woods and musks finish out the fragrance, leaving the intrigue of smoky woods to linger on your skin.

#6860. Hublot Just Relaunched Its First Watch. Founder Carlo Crocco Explains How It Changed Everything.

In Milan, celebrating the release of the Classic Fusion Tributo, I got to catch up with the guy who altered the aesthetics of modern watchmaking.

If you spend a lot of time following the world of watches, Hublot’s origin story might feel as familiar as one of the fairytales you heard at bedtime as a child. If you don’t, though, it might feel just as fantastical as some yarn about a princess who feels a pea under a stack of mattresses or a prince who slays a fire-breathing dragon.

You start with an unlikely hero. This time around, it’s an Italian entrepreneur with a big, bold, brazen idea. He wants to make a watch, something unlike anyone has ever seen. It’ll be gold, with an angular case shaped like the porthole on a ship. Not so crazy yet. But wait. Because, breaking entirely with the conventions of the day, the strap won’t be made of exotic leather or precious metal but rubber. Humble, industrial rubber.

This is exactly what Hublot founder Carlo Crocco envisioned nearly half a century ago, and what he brought to the Basel Watch Fair—pretty much the place to be if you wanted to be a part of the business of timekeeping—in 1980. He spent three years developing the idea, all without doing a bit of market research. He just believed in his vision. And then the fair began.

“I was very anxious, because for the first two days, no one came into our booth,” he says. But the anxiety didn’t have long to fester. Word was spreading. And then? People galore. “On the third day, we realized that everyone was speaking about us because it was really something different from all the other watches.”

That first watch—36mm in diameter, with a quartz movement and a black rubber strap—quickly became a hit. Industry buzz gave way to the kind of clientele most brands can only imagine. The King of Greece bought one in Gstaad for himself and another for his brother-in-law, the King of Spain. More royals followed. (“By the way, they’re all related,” Crocco quips.)

But the biggest thing wasn’t getting insiders to a booth at a trade fair or convincing kings to strap Hublot watches—named, if you’re curious, for the French word for porthole—on their wrists. It was changing how the industry thought about the way watches are made.

“At that time, the mentality of watchmaking was very classical,” Crocco says. “They couldn’t think, normal people, of putting together rubber and gold. I personally thought it was a really good idea because it was against the current. It was very risky, but if you don’t risk, you have no result.”

The result was a watch industry that completely recalibrated itself. Crocco says that within four or five years of the original Hublot’s release, the brand was already dealing with imitators. Now, the idea of combining disparate materials is firmly embedded in the minds of both consumers and designers. You can see it on watches from brands that sell at the local department store and brands that only sell to folks who haven’t seen the inside of one of those since the first time their private jet took off. Crocco’s big idea changed the whole game.

Which means it’s a pretty good time to go back to the beginning of this thing. Last month in Milan, Hublot debuted a new take on that first, groundbreaking watch. Dubbed the Hublot Classic Fusion Tributo, it’s an homage but not a nuts-and-bolts recreation. Instead of the original 36mm case, it’s been upsized to a more 2026-appropriate 38mm. And instead of a quartz movement—Crocco thought it was “more practical” at the time but has come around to the enduring appeal of mechanical construction—a self-winding HUB1711 movement with a 50-hour power reserve keeps things ticking along. Oh, and now you can get it in white gold, too.

Then there’s that rubber strap. When Crocco designed the original Hublot, he was insistent that the strap was entirely straight; he felt it was crucial to the aesthetic proposition. On the Tributo, it’s ever-so-slightly, almost imperceptibly tapered, a necessary concession to keep the proportions intact after increasing the case size (which, handily, lets that automatic movement sit comfortably inside).

But, as with the first Hublot, it’s not all about tech specs and watch-nerd particulars. It’s about the spirit of the thing. And the spirit of this watch stays true to that pioneering design. There are twelve screws around the bezel, so you can keep an eye on the hour despite the dial’s lack of indices—something Crocco says was a non-negotiable when he started consulting on the new release. The yellow gold version keeps the abyss-like black lacquer dial and the white gold throws us a tiny (and welcome) curveball by using onyx. The combo of precious metal and rubber still hits, 46 years later.

If you prod him how he feels about the way that he shifted the watch industry, Crocco won’t brag. He left Hublot nearly 20 years ago, when it sold to LVMH. He knows his idea changed things; he’s happy about it. But this isn’t his day job anymore. Still, when he’s asked if he could be considered the godfather of fusing high and low in watchmaking—and you give him a minute to ponder it—he’ll tell you the truth.

#6859. Utility Jeans Are the Coolest Denim Trend to Wear Right Now

They take you from practical to polished.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Often when we think about it, some of the most-worn pieces in our closets involve some form of practicality. Perhaps it’s that reversible raincoat, that cozy office-to-weekend cardigan, or a pair of utility jeans, which you may also know as cargo or work jeans.

When it comes to practicality and nonchalance, few pieces deliver quite like this one. Deep pockets, sturdy hardware, and a relaxed, easy fit mean you can run errands, commute, or settle in at a café wearing a polished version of your favorite work pants. The best pairs look just as good dressed up with a sleek knit and boots as they do dressed down with a favorite tee and sneakers, and they only get better with wear. Consider them proof that the hardest-working item in your closet can also be the most stylish.

Whether you’re after a wide-leg carpenter, a slouchy cargo, or a straight leg with just a few well-placed pockets, the right pair is the one that makes getting dressed easier. Ahead, the best utility jeans to add to your fall rotation, for a seamless blend of function and style.

This utility jean knows how to work it. Adjustable straps at the hem let you dial the tapered barrel leg up or down, proving the statement detail is as functional as it is cool. Plus, it has plenty of pockets throughout, so your favorite lip balm and other little essentials are always close at hand.

Beyond the perfect neutral shade, these jeans feature utilitarian patch pockets and knee darts that create a curvy, cohesive shape. Though roomy through the thigh and rise, they still read as sleek and slim.

A relaxed boyfriend fit and a cropped leg give way to a jean that can easily be dressed up or down with the slightest changes. Its utilitarian shape, complete with roomy patch pockets and a sturdy, broken-in feel, works just as well with a crisp white button-down and loafers as it does with a graphic tee and sneakers.

A barrel cargo is the denim combination we never knew we needed. With a dark wash, baggy fit, and snap-button pockets, this pair feels relaxed yet elevated.

We love a cuffed hem, and this particular jean does one so well. Contrasting soft gold thread runs throughout, bringing a subtle, workwear-inspired polish, while darting at the knee gives a sculpted, slightly curved effect.

With silver rivet accents and deep patch pockets, this straight-leg carpenter pant is the dressed-up cousin of the classic utility jean.

There’s something so thrilling about finding a pair of chic utility pants in a light tan shade. Made of 100 percent cotton twill, this pair is non-stretch and lightweight, making it a must for busy weekday afternoons and weekends alike.

These olive green utility jeans pair oversize pockets with a soft barrel leg for a look that’s equal parts practical and polished.

#6853. Rails Is the Cool Fashion Brand Celebrities Wear—and Editors Gift

Give something cozy and luxe from the label Taylor Swift and J.Lo love.

Department stores have plenty of gifts, but narrowing down endless options can be overwhelming. For a more streamlined search, fashion editors often stick to browsing a single trusted brand with a smaller, more thoughtfully curated selection, like Rails. Worn by celebrities like Taylor Swift, Beyoncé, Jennifer Lopez, and Hilary Duff, the California-based contemporary fashion label is one of our top picks for holiday gift shopping.

Rails built its reputation on casual plaid shirts, but its sweaters, jackets, dresses, and casual tops have kept fashion insiders coming back for more than a decade. From cardigans and coats to winter accessories, the brand’s pieces have a luxurious feel without being too precious. (Most are priced under $300, yet look like they cost double.) These aren’t cashmere and silk items recipients will save for special occasions; they’re pieces they’ll rely on for everyday wear.

Rails sweaters, beanies, scarves, and other cold-weather essentials come in a range of neutral colors you can mix and match into coordinating gift sets. Knit from a plush cashmere-silk blend, the Miranda sweater, Alps scarf, Moritz mittens, and Autumn beanie (or any combination of the four) make foolproof gifts for the stylish women on your list. The brand’s elevated fabrics and quality construction are enough to impress on their own, but its celebrity fans give you a fun tidbit to share with recipients, too.

Should anything catch your eye for your own wardrobe, we fully support treating yourself to a little something, too. The Franca dress that Taylor Swift recently wore to dinner in Kansas City is still in stock, so you might want to peruse the label for potential date-night and holiday party outfits, too.

#6852. After I Kicked Drugs and Alcohol, I Found a New Addiction: Cheating on My Fiancée

Thirteen years of sobriety taught me that the most dangerous thing for an addict isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And I keep getting away with it.

Ethical nonmonogamy relies on a foundation of total transparency. Partners openly agree to terms and continually negotiate them together. Betrayals such as infidelity can take many shapes and break the agreement a couple has made, monogamous or not. For people in recovery, however, the adrenaline of secret keeping can feel familiar. Clinicians call this behavioral, or process, addiction—in which compulsion lies in the thrill of the chase rather than the payoff. It doesn’t excuse betrayal, but it can help explain how a person committed to honesty ends up living a lie.

For this installment of the Secret Lives of Men, we spoke to Owen,* a 36-year-old brand strategist with 13 years of sobriety who spent most of the first two years of his relationship cheating on the partner he plans to marry. His story begs the question: What does it mean to carry out “living amends,” an Alcoholics Anonymous term for permanently changing your behavior for the better, when you aren’t ready to confess?

*Names and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

Once a week, my fiancée has a therapy session over a video call that she takes from our bedroom. We both work from home, so while that happens, I’m usually barricaded in my office with noise-canceling headphones on, keeping up with work. Recently, I came out to the kitchen to pour a coffee after her hour was up, and she wasn’t home.

She takes walks, so there was no reason to panic. But my mind went where it has gone a lot lately: She found out. By whatever cruel trick of fate, she knew, and she was out there walking it off, letting the anger curdle. When she came back, she would bring hell with her.

I called once. She didn’t pick up. I didn’t try again. For half an hour, I paced the apartment unable to get a single thing done. I checked my phone every few seconds. I rehearsed what I’d say when she walked through the door: which parts I’d confess, which parts I’d still try to save. I worried myself sick. When she finally walked back in, it turned out she’d just been on a long phone call with her sister. She took off her headset, kissed me hello, and asked what I had in mind for dinner. My heart kept pounding long after she’d walked into the kitchen.

Until two years ago, I could say with real pride that I had never cheated on anyone. Not once. Not even when I was drinking and doing all those drugs, back when I lied about nearly everything else. I’d had opportunities, but infidelity was the one line I never crossed.

Advanced 12-step work taught me a strange paradox: Sometimes you have to sit with the agonizing truth that you are doing something wrong. That you aren’t ready or able to stop. I’ve built 13 years of sobriety on the idea that we are only as sick as our secrets. But I should admit something up front: I’m good at telling stories, especially about myself. Even now, I catch myself deciding which parts to share. So here it is: For the better part of two years, I cheated on the woman I’m going to marry. She still doesn’t know.

In recovery, you learn that the most dangerous thing that can happen to a person with an addiction isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And every time you skate by, the lie gets a little easier to tell. The disease gets a little more room to grow. I first learned that at 23. I had to learn it again in my 30s.

I come from a long line of talented, tortured people. My father sold commercial real estate and spent years in and out of a quiet, high-functioning addiction to painkillers. He went away for treatment twice. My mother was raised by an alcoholic and carried a lot of anger. My parents divorced when I was young, both remarried badly, and neither of their houses was a safe place emotionally.

At 19, while in college, I fell into a relationship I can only describe as wickedly codependent. We went through two miscarriages together and became trauma bonded. I didn’t know how to grieve, so I was drinking heavily and taking almost anything I could find: stimulants, psychedelics, dissociatives. Just before I turned 21, I caught my girlfriend in a lie so profound it ripped the wool off my eyes: She had been sleeping with my best friend for nearly the entire time we’d been together. One of the miscarried babies was actually his. I ended things the day I found out and took the situation as the perfect excuse to keep killing myself slowly.

I didn’t make it a year out of college before the consequences caught up with me. The fall after graduation, I was driving home from a concert when I got pulled over for a dead taillight. I was relatively sober and polite and was sure I’d talked my way out of it—right up until the officer asked me to empty my pockets. Inside were a bag of powder that I’d been promised was Molly and a few pills I didn’t have a prescription for. They booked me and sent everything off to the state lab. For three months, nothing happened. Meanwhile, I painted myself further into the corner before the floor ultimately fell out. When the charge finally came, I began a year and a half of court dates and probation.

As a first offender, I pled into a court-mandated, recovery-focused diversion program with regular drug tests. I followed the program to a T, at least for a while.

One weekend, when house-sitting alone for a neighbor, I took a drink out of boredom and pure spite. No test caught it. Then I lied about it to my counselors, the court, and my family. A month later, guilt finally won out, and I admitted it to my sponsor. A few days later, he brought it back up and told me I had to come clean to everyone.

“The fact that you were willing to conceal that from everyone for this long,” he said, “is the only evidence you need of how sick you are.”

The problem wasn’t the drink. It was how easily I hid it. I surrendered to the program and handed my life over to a higher power. I haven’t had a drink since.

I went celibate by choice for three years. I did a lot of therapy and learned that I had swung from codependent to avoidant. I had finally regained control, and I didn’t want to hand myself to anyone. Then, in my late 20s, I decided to give dating another shot.

Before long, I leaned into ethical nonmonogamy and dated as “solo poly,” which means I was seeing multiple people without a primary partner. Casual dating became my primary hobby. Some connections were purely physical; some were friends with benefits; some went emotionally deep. At my peak, I was sleeping with six people, two or three times a week. I felt I was winning at something. I was always honest with every partner about who I was, sobriety included. But I wasn’t honest with myself about why I needed so much of it.

Then, later in therapy, I came to understand that the dopamine that keeps people in an addiction loop is driven less by the thing itself than by the anticipation of getting it. Dating apps are built on that anticipation. For me, there were three hits: the match, the first nudes, the first hookup. In the program, we say the disease of addiction wears as many faces as it needs to. The chase was one of those faces.

I matched with Maya as she was getting ready to leave on a six-week work trip to Thailand. We messaged far longer than usual, trading long voice notes across the 11-hour time difference—her from hotel lobbies at night, me on morning walks. By the time we finally met, I already knew how she laughed. Our first date was electric, the best of my life. We sat at the bar until they started stacking chairs, talking about family, grief, books that had saved us. She was self-sufficient and emotionally deep. She was great at conversation. She was beautiful.

A few months into our relationship, we curled up on her couch on a Sunday afternoon and talked about ethical nonmonogamy. We’d both dabbled in it, but neither of us had done it inside a primary relationship. I told her I wanted it. She was open to it. We agreed to start monogamous and build the foundation first, but we never said for how long.

The truth is, our sex wasn’t great. Early on, I suggested something new in bed, and she went quiet and turned toward the wall. Neither of us brought it up the next morning. I internalized the idea that I couldn’t ask for what I wanted, that this was just how it would be until we opened things up. Instead of communicating, I let the resentment build.

Then, one afternoon at a coffee shop across town, I ran into Bree, a woman I used to hook up with. She had been the perfect friend with benefits: incredible chemistry but no desire for a relationship. We stayed and talked for an hour. When she mentioned she lived ten minutes away, neither of us pretended not to know what that meant. By that evening, I was at her place. It was the first time in my life I had been unfaithful in any way, and I felt awful. When I went home, I waited for the consequences. They never came.

A big part of addiction is justification. We were planning to be nonmonogamous, I told myself, so the way I’d just conducted myself was basically equivalent. It wasn’t that bad. I can see now how thin that logic was. Nonmonogamy runs on transparency and consent. I was keeping a secret.

Over the next year and a half, my infidelities came and went. A few more times with Bree. A handful of times with anonymous women in other cities. Trans women, sex workers. I traveled for work, and I took advantage of the distance. I kept my phone face down, hid dating apps, used end-to-end-encrypted texting. For each time I followed through, there were five times I stopped myself. Each close call made me feel like I had it under control.

Strangely, the woman I cheated with most also kept me from making worse mistakes. Bree became my confidante, the only person I was fully honest with about any of it. One night, I arranged to meet a sex worker. I was sitting in my car outside the hotel when clarity hit. The only thing I could think to do was call Bree. She talked me down. She and I ended up having sex that night. The program has a phrase for that kind of thinking: I want what I want when I want it.

Meanwhile, the relationship I was betraying kept getting better. We slowly began to practice nonmonogamy, and it took us far longer than either of us expected. We listened to the audiobook version of Polysecure by Jessica Fern together. We’d pause so often to talk that a half-hour chapter took two hours. It gave me a way to ask for what I wanted without it feeling like an attack on her. One night, Maya paused the book and asked me, plainly, how she could do better. For the first time, I believed she meant it, and I told her. The sex got better. Then, much better.

The final close call came in June. Bored at my desk, I connected with someone on an anonymous hookup site, and we moved to texting. A few messages in, after I’d sent a couple of nudes, my phone lit up with a long blackmail threat. Attached was a screenshot of Maya’s Instagram. This kind of thing had happened four or five times before, back when I was single. Go ahead, I would say. I never considered paying, and no one ever followed through. But this time, I closed my laptop, walked to the bathroom, sat on the edge of the tub until my hands stopped shaking, and blocked the number. This time, I had something to lose. I was forced to face how stupid I was being.

I shared the story in therapy. My therapist, who usually lets me do most of the talking, leaned back, looked at his clipboard, and said, “What the fuck are you doing? This is like the 20th time something like this has happened.”

I didn’t have an answer. Nothing had ever happened to me. That was the whole point.

This past August, on a weekend trip to the Catskills with friends, I proposed to Maya. She said yes before I could finish the question. I meant every word I said that weekend.

Maya and I have now officially opened our relationship. We’ve mostly played as a couple. When she sees someone on her own, she tells me everything the next morning. Last month, I sexted with a woman for days and chose not to tell her. Though I have permission, I can’t seem to give up the secrecy.

Growing up in the houses I did, I hid the truth to keep the peace. Recovery taught me to say it anyway, but I’ve only ever practiced with people who can’t leave, like a sponsor or a therapist. Telling Maya is different. Sometimes, I reverse the question: What if she were the one carrying a secret like this? I like to think that I’d find a way to accept it, because through everything I’ve done, I have never loved her any less.

In the program, when you can’t make amends without causing further harm, you make what’s called living amends. It starts with stopping the behavior. Then you try to love better, act cleaner, and stay present day after day, without asking for absolution. That’s where I am now. I pray regularly that this pain is my consequence. If that’s my penance, I’ll carry it.

#6849. The 2026 Way to Style Boots and Jeans Is Easier Than You Think

The options are endless, and the results are timeless.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

If we could only choose two wardrobe staples to wear for the rest of our lives (okay, maybe just for the rest of this season), boots and jeans would be our top picks. The combinations are endless: You could opt for wide-leg baggy jeans or Parisian stovepipe jeans; a kitten-heel ankle boot or an over-the-knee option. The looks you can create are endless, but there are still a few standout silhouettes that work especially well.

Whether you’re getting ready for the office, a date, a casual evening, or a fancier occasion, there’s a way to pair boots and jeans for any dress code. Below, we found the five best ways to style boots with jeans in 2026.

It depends on exactly how slim your straight-leg jeans are, but for the most part, you shouldn’t have any trouble pairing the silhouette with boots (unless they have an extra-wide calf). Moto boots add an edgy element to an otherwise polished striped button-down.

Cropped lengths can be perfect for showcasing your favorite boots. Regardless of hemlines, consider tucking one or both pant legs into your boots for an especially high-fashion styling.

This is my preferred way of styling jeans and boots. I can’t help it—I love a chunky shoe, and I’m partial to my Dr. Martens. I’d skip her open-front jacket and instead opt for a boatneck top adorned with a brooch for a pop of color.

Kick flares and boots go together like peanut butter and jelly. Try an all-black ensemble and step into a pair of pointed-toe stilettos for a look that goes from day to night.

Boot is literally in the name here, so how could you resist pairing these two when they’re so clearly meant to be? This look feels very Carolyn Bessette Kennedy to me, don’t you think?

#6848. Esquire First Look: How Bad Monkey Season 2 Brews Up Even More Trouble in Paradise

Creator and showrunner Bill Lawrence reveals how John Malkovich’s love of the Apple TV crime series starring Vince Vaughn landed him a role as the “comedic and dangerous” new villain.

For Bill Lawrence, producing some of TV’s biggest comedies is serious business. Once the architect of cable juggernauts Scrubs and Cougar Town, the 57-year-old producer is now at the helm of several streaming-era darlings, including Ted Lasso, Shrinking, Rooster, and Bad Monkey. But of all his shows, Lawrence tells Esquire that Bad Monkey scratches a specific itch.

“My favorite movies, books, and TV shows that grabbed me as a kid melted genres,” Lawrence says. A self-professed diehard for buddy-cop classics like 48 Hrs. and Beverly Hills Cop, the two-time Emmy winner says that the films’ signature mixture of humor with grittier themes and settings made them more than just funny movies. “People remember them as raucous comedies, and they were. But there were real emotional stakes,” says Lawrence. “And the first author that got me into that stuff was Carl Hiaasen.”

A political journalist and author from Florida (where Lawrence’s family originates), Carl Hiaasen wrote the satirical crime novel Bad Monkey in 2013. The book was adapted in full in 2024 as the Apple TV series’ first season. Lawrence was, and is, a “massive fan” of Hiaasen’s. “Then I got to know this guy,” he says. “That one of my heroes turned out to be like I hoped he would be, and that he let me take one of his best properties and turn it into a little bit of throwback TV—it’s one of the most fun rides of my career.”

Now Lawrence is ready to unpack the upcoming second season of Bad Monkey—and its Emmy-worthy newcomers—which returns to Apple TV on December 2. A new trailer, launching today, is embedded just below.

Bad Monkey season 2 sees Vince Vaughn returns as Yancy, a former Miami detective turned reluctant restaurant inspector who lives in the Florida Keys. With severed arms and medical fraud deep in his rearview mirror, Yancy finds himself caught in a new case involving a shady restaurant—an obvious front for a criminal enterprise—and the underworld boss behind it. Three-time Emmy Award winner and Oscar nominee John Malkovich joins as a new antagonist, Spencer. While Lawrence resists revealing too much about Spencer, he describes the role as a “comedic and dangerous” villain who drives Yancy to the brink.

“One of my favorite things about Carl’s books is that they’re really, at their core, character stories,” Lawrence says. “Spencer” is a role that Lawrence and his team “reverse-engineered” for Malkovich when they caught wind of the actor praising Bad Monkey while promoting his 2025 film Opus. “Randomly, Malkovich said, ‘My favorite show this year was Bad Monkey,’ ” Lawrence recalls. “This story started from a place of [thinking], If John Malkovich was kind and silly enough to bring him in this world, who would he be? You will meet him as our other fearless leader this year.”

The second season of Bad Monkey breaks from Hiaasen’s books (with the author’s blessing) to tell a new story that, in a spiritual sense, takes place between the original Bad Monkey novel and its 2016 sequel, Razor Girl. “We took massive liberties expanding certain characters, making the world bigger,” Lawrence says about collaborating with Hiaasen. “My nightmare would be to do anything he didn’t feel was true to Florida or his voice. I’m not overstepping when I tell you he helped come up with the main issue that Rosa’s chasing down [in season 2], because it was something he had written about when he was a journalist.”

Adds Lawrence, “It was nerve-racking. One of the scariest things, if you’re doing an IP [series] and it’s your favorite author, [is when you] have to make up things, but I’m very lucky he’s become a close friend and is candid with me to tell me if we hit or miss on it.”

Malkovich isn’t the only new face in season 2. A slew of stand-up comics, up-and-coming actors, and notable names fill out the ensemble. Sam Jaeger, Zavior Phillips, Peter Billingsley, Sofia Boutella, and Mo Amer all appear in the second season, to name a few. The Handmaid’s Tale’s Yvonne Strahovski also joins as a series regular in a role Lawrence hints has “something much bigger and deeper going on.” Returning characters from season 1 include Rosa (Natalie Martinez), Ro (John Ortiz), and Caitlin (Charlotte Lawrence, Bill’s real-life daughter).

But Bad Monkey is still all about Vince Vaughn’s Yancy, the endlessly witty, if also damaged, soul of the whole shebang. Yancy has changed a little since season 1—“He realized that he was a catalyst for change in other people,” Lawrence says—but he’s also the same dude who knows how to relax in his own backyard. “He was still drifting around and ended up very alone. Were we to have him be the same loner throughout season 2, it would be a mistake. He is the same Yancy. But watching that character decide what it means to let people into his life—whether it be Caitlin or Rosa—that is his personal journey. I wouldn’t want to watch the show if it weren’t Yancy being Yancy on some level.”

Yancy’s big story for season 2 involves his father, Jim, played again by Scott Glenn, whom Yancy invites to stay with him in his home. Lawrence says this story aims to highlight Yancy’s internal struggle as a budding father figure. “We all have dynamics with our fathers—me, Vince, [co-executive producer] Matt [Tarses]. You become your parent’s ... I don’t want to say caretaker, but the roles switch. That’s a role that comes your way. The person who always could take care of you, you need to step up and do that for them and embrace taking their role for everyone else.”

“If you ask me what [season 2] is actually about,” says Lawrence, “It’s about Yancy stepping in to be the patriarch of whatever his found family is, and whether or not he is ready to do that.”

If one thing sets Bad Monkey apart from Lawrence’s other works, it’s action. Lawrence aims to pay proper homage to the movies that influenced him with “feature-level” set pieces this year. “In season 1, we were flying by the seat of our pants, seeing what we could do, especially in Florida,” says Lawrence. “The one thing we wanted to upgrade in season 2 is the action. It’s stuff I haven’t shot in my normal 30-minute comedies before, and I think we pulled it off.”

The action will be felt through the characters—the ones standing, anyway. Bill Lawrence teases that the sheer amount of newcomers means one or two or several won’t make it to the end. “One of the things about Carl’s books is that when you reach the end, a lot of the regulars have died. Knowing the cast will never get too big, it’s fun to see the smoke clear and [find out] who’s left.”

This is serious business. But it’s still a blast.

#6846. Michael J. Fox Shares the Best Advice His Father Ever Gave Him

“My parents were risk averse,” the 65-year-old Back to the Future actor says. “But I wanted to do all this crazy stuff.”

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Michael J. Fox is a fighter. When the Back to the Future and Spin City actor was diagnosed with Parkinson’s disease in 1991, he refused to let the condition cut his life or his career short. He kept acting, earned some Emmy Awards and Golden Globes statuettes, and most recently appeared on season 3 of Apple TV’s Shrinking with Harrison Ford.

As the 65-year-old actor tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned,” his drive all stems from a quote his father told him when he left Canada to pursue acting in America: “Well, if you’re going to be a lumberjack, you might as well go to the goddamn forest.”

Though Fox’s father, a former Canadian Armed Forces soldier, was likely talking about Hollywood, Fox says that he took his advice to mean that if you’re going to do anything in life—whether it be his acting, his activism, or even his sheer optimism in the face of disease—you better fully engage yourself in it.

“My parents were born into the Depression ... so they were risk averse. Their whole life was just to have a nice life that didn’t leave anything exposed,” Fox continues. “And I wanted to do all this crazy stuff.” Namely Teen Wolf, three Back to the Future films, playing onstage with Coldplay, poking fun at his condition on Curb Your Enthusiasm (“because it’s hilarious”), and never letting Parkinson’s slow him down.

“I went through eight years of keeping it to myself and trying to forestall it with exercise and diet, learn a little bit from the doctors and experts, and then when I decided to share it it was like this great slap on the forehead,” Fox says about why he formed the Michael J. Fox Foundation. “I thought, There’s all these people that are experiencing this. Why aren’t we sharing our information about it?”

Elsewhere in the episode, Fox shares what he’s learned from Rush Limbaugh accusing him of faking his diagnosis, meeting Barack Obama before he was president, and raising roughly $3 billion for Parkinson’s research. You can watch the episode above, watch it on YouTube here, or listen to it on Spotify and Apple Podcasts.

“I don’t look at any time [in my life] and say, ‘Who was I then?’ or ‘Who will I be in a week?’ or “Who am I now?’ ” Fox says. “I just put my head down enough that I could still see where I was going and just plow ahead. And I realize now that I’m in this great position where I can have an effect on people’s lives.”
