#6764. Presidents, Bullfighters, and California—Elaine de Kooning Painted Whatever She Wanted

And you can see it all at a new retrospective at Berry Campbell gallery in New York

In the history books, Elaine de Kooning is often eclipsed by her husband, the famed painter Willem. This fate befell the women in the Abstract Expressionist movement, including Lee Krasner, Grace Hartigan, Joan Mitchell, and Helen Frankenthaler, who became known as the “Ninth Street Women,” all of whom received their flowers after decades of being overshadowed by their male counterparts. But you simply cannot tell the story of mid-century art—and Abstract Expressionism—without Elaine.

De Kooning, who died in 1989 at 70, sat at the epicenter of the art world for five decades. She deftly balanced two parallel practices in portraiture and abstract expressionism, unafraid to allow these genres to intermingle. Beyond her painting, she founded the artist collective The Club in Greenwich Village and wrote art criticism for Art News, celebrating artists like Arshile Gorky and Jackson Pollock. President John F. Kennedy, New York artist Toni Ross, former Art News editor-in-chief Thomas Hess, and American painter Alex Katz are among the artist’s impressive roster of sitters. A number of these portraits currently hang at New York’s Berry Campbell gallery. Each image frames its subject with expressive brushwork and vivid, bright colors. Among these works, most featuring starry figures, is a young boy in a cowboy hat, featured in the painting Jemp #1 (1963). This little kid is De Kooning’s nephew, and the reason the show exists.

Gallerists Martha Campbell and Christine Berry connected with Chris Luyckx, the nephew, who had preserved De Kooning’s paintings for decades and felt her work deserved renewed attention. By the time they met, roughly a decade had passed since De Kooning’s last notable show, a retrospective at the National Portrait Gallery in 2015, followed by smaller shows that focused on specific periods of her life. “By doing these individual shows, you miss the incredible throughline of her work from the late 1940s until the day she died in 1989,” says Campbell. “It goes with our ethos of always believing in the artist from the very beginning until the very end.” Berry Campbell’s show, simply titled “Elaine De Kooning” and on view through December 19, celebrates the artist in her entirety, featuring around 30 works sourced directly from the family’s collection

De Kooning’s oeuvre retains its wonder by evading classification, collapsing boundaries between portraiture and abstract expressionism to forge a distinct visual sensibility. Part of her practice fit firmly in the world of abstract art. Paintings like Untitled (1960) reveled in obscuring the world around her; here, she depicts New York’s verticality. “Elaine de Kooning was an abstract expressionist, but as a woman artist, she didn’t have dealers breathing down her neck saying, ‘Paint more of what we see as Abstract Expressionism,” Campbell says, noting that this provided the artist with wiggle room.

This should not overshadow her exceptional command of pure abstraction. The 11-foot-wide California (1961), a barrage of vivid colors underscoring De Kooning’s compositional mastery, sits at the very back of the gallery. Berry affirms “it is the most important piece in the exhibition,” noting that “there is no other painting of that scale or magnitude or from that time period on the market, because very other painting that is like that is in a museum collection.” Doubling down on its consequence, Campbell recalls, “There’s a great note that we found from [legendary art dealer] Joan Washburn that says that when she was her dealer, she said, ‘This is the best painting that Elaine de Kooning has ever painted.’”

Elements of her abstract work often defined her portraiture. Energetic bursts of bright paint consumed her subjects. Tom Hess (1963), for instance, features the titular journalist among flurries of orange, yellow, and red paint, leaving most of the canvas untouched. “She was an abstract expressionist, but within her own definition of what that was, so she used these abstract expressionist brushstrokes to glorify what she loved.”

De Kooning loved her subjects, occasionally obsessing over one topic and painting it repeatedly, ranging from bullfighting and baseball to the painter Aristodimos Kaldis and President Kennedy. This new show highlights all of these hyperfocused periods, including a standout from her paintings of the former president. Her connection to him started in 1962, when Kennedy sat for De Kooning in Palm Beach to complete a commission for the Harry S. Truman Presidential Library & Museum. The fixation intensified after the president’s assassination in 1963. JFK #7 (1963), on view at the gallery, is only one example of a year’s worth of works.

“She did get fixated on JFK, because she was painting him and then he was shot,” Berry explains. “That was pretty shocking. She painted him for a full year without stopping. People only focus on JFK. But she did portraits from the beginning, in the 1940s, all the way through. She did self-portraits, as well as portraits of her neighbors and her boss, Tom Hess. The portraits are a through line of her career.”

A sophisticated command of color is evident across De Kooning’s entire body of work. Bright colors clash in her abstract works, and in her portraiture, color builds texture. Perhaps most surprising, however, is that De Kooning often avoided looking at color when creating her images. For instance, she preferred to use black-and-white newspaper clippings as references for her basketball paintings, imbuing them with color guided by her personal sensibility. “Color is key to what she does,” Berry explains, pointing to the portrait of poet Frank Lima, a friend with a drug problem whom De Kooning renders in muted greens.

One painting, Bacchus #20-G (1983), an image of Jules Dalou’s The Triumph of Silenus painted six years before her death, epitomizes what’s so striking about De Kooning’s paintings. The lush painting features the sculpture’s twisted bodies, rendered in an opulent violet, with a backdrop of verdant green. It demonstrates her capacity to seamlessly balance portraiture and Abstract Expressionism, a testament to her unwavering devotion to her distinct visual language. This vision, after years on the fringe, finally receives the flower it deserves, alongside, rather than below, her husband.

#6763. Silver Fox at 16. How Men Are Embracing Gray Hair at Every Age

At 16, James Chudley’s first gray hair was spotted by the girl sitting next to him in Biology. He thought it was a joke. She took a photo to prove it. There it was: one silver thread, just above his ear. “For the rest of the year,” he says, “every Biology lesson, it was: ‘Oh, there’s another one!’”

Chudley, now a personal trainer-in-training from Surrey, England wasn’t too bothered. “I thought I’d just pluck it out,” he says. By 19, though, there were too many to pluck. An anti-gray shampoo turned his bath blue; dye was another option, but he had reservations: “I didn’t want to end up looking like Dracula.”

Away from school, Chudley gradually got comfortable with his grays. Even so, he mentioned them before anyone else could, “almost as a defense mechanism.” Usually the response was playful roasting, sometimes even a compliment. But then someone would catch him off guard. Chudley, now 22, was recently working as a delivery driver when a customer looked him up and down. “Bit young to be going gray, isn’t it?” she said. Almost as an afterthought, she added, “Is it stressing you out?”

Gray hair is an inevitability for most men. Live long enough, and the color starts to drain. For many people, that begins in their early 30s and becomes more visible with age. By 50, suggests a study by the British Association of Dermatologists, up to 23% of us will have half a head of gray hair. The science is straightforward. Hair follicles contain melanocytes, the cells responsible for producing pigment. As we age, the stem cells that replenish them become less active, and the color fades. It’s like a printer cartridge running out of ink: the paper keeps coming, but eventually, there’s nothing on there.

Going gray in your teens and early 20s, however, is another matter. It’s relatively uncommon, although anecdotes are easy to find. Spend five minutes on TikTok and you’ll see plenty asking why they’re suddenly gray. Reddit has versions too, with users swapping stories and theories about why they—and seemingly everyone around them—are losing their color so young. “I don’t know if you’ve noticed, but people’s hair seems to be turning white really fast lately,” says one post. “How did we end up in the worst century where literally anything can go wrong?”

It’s hard to say if we’re actually going gray earlier, or if it’s just that more people are talking about it. The theories, meanwhile, keep on coming—from mineral deficiencies to heavy-metal exposure. There’s also an optimistic explanation: More people are open to embracing it.

Young men are spending more money than ever on looking good—even on their grays. Rather than shopping for cheap cover-ups, they are forensically researching science-backed cosmetics. Part of this also means a rejection of chemical-heavy products and a more skeptical attitude towards unnecessary cosmetics. Hair dye, after all, is not entirely harmless.

Online communities like “Gray and Proud” and “Silver Revolution” have tens of thousands of members. Paul Mescal and Jeremy Allen White have also done their part for gray-hair representation among young(ish) people: The Times dubbed them “silver fox cubs.”

Maybe things really are shifting. But if the demand for anti-gray products is any indication, the revolution is still a way off. Anti-gray serums are becoming big business. The global market is expected to jump from around $1.3 billion to $2 billion by 2031, according to Deep Market Insights. Cosmetics Business magazine calls it the “next men’s grooming trend,” with growing interest in peptide-based products. On the internet, you see both sides. On Reddit, a 26-year-old guy shares a photo of his gray head of hair, saying that he’s already got more than his dad. The top comment reads: “Do your due diligence, of course—bloodwork, diet etc.” Someone else follows up with: “[Guys] with gray hair are [hot].”

One person making the case for Gen Z’s grey-haired men is model and musician Charlie Jeer. The 24-year-old first found fame on the Netflix dating show Too Hot to Handle. He has the chiseled jawline and cheekbones of a conventional heartthrob, but there’s another feature viewers can’t seem to get past: his hair. “I feel like it gets brought up in conversation every single day in my life,” he says.

Jeer first noticed the grays as a teenager, and mostly shrugged off the comments. Online, it’s a different story. “Why are you going gray?”; “Can’t tell if bro is 15 or 60; ” “Err this old man.” “It’s relentless,” he says.

Now, Jeer sees the grays as an opportunity to stand out. They’ve helped build his profile as a model, too, partly because of the “age ambiguity” they give him. He’s picked up credits with brands like Davidoff and Adidas. “There’s a massive commercial potential for me to make a black hair dye and just start marketing that,” he says. “I never did because I was like, ‘No, I’m proud of my hair the way it is.’”

On TikTok, Jeer takes on his critics with captions like: “Joke’s on them when I’m a silver fox by 30.” He really likes it when he hears from other young guys going gray. “I’ve had a little gray-haired community of people come forward and present themselves,” he says. “It’s nice to hear someone else say that they’ve had people come at them for being gray-haired and that they’re going to continue to wear it with confidence.”

Jeer might be on the young end of the gray-hair population, but as a guy, he’s riding a well-worn track. Men have always been afforded the privilege of associating their grays with looking more distinguished. You don’t have to be George Clooney (or a Too Hot to Handle contestant) to benefit from that. Women, meanwhile, aren’t part of that deal: gray hair is more often simply read as old, an obviously unfair double standard.

It still come with baggage for men, though: age, lost virility, fading relevance. “The first gray” is practically a trope in its own right—a visual shorthand in films, books and TV for a character beginning to age, often followed by the inevitable midlife crisis. There are dozens of those scenes, in everything from The Simpsons to Vanilla Sky.

Matt Newman is a New York–based hairstylist who hates his gray hair. “There are so many examples of men with premature grays that people love and adore,” he says. “But in your 20s, that might not be an aesthetic you’re ready to dive into.”

Perhaps that’s why Jackass star Johnny Knoxville waited until 49 to finally embrace his natural hair color. He started going gray in his 20s and dyed it for most of his life. Then, during lockdown, he asked his wife to give him a buzz cut to reveal the silver underneath, on Instagram. “I should start a challenge but I don’t want to call anyone out,” he wrote on Instagram. Going gray, declared one Mr. Porter headline, was now a “punk power move”.

That’s not quite how Saavan Varambhia, 25, a data analyst from Buckinghamshire, would have described it. Not at first, anyway. Like Chudley, he was at school when a group of female friends spotted his grays and tried to pluck them out. “I was like, ‘Hmm, I don’t know how I feel about this,’” he says. A few years later, he had enough gray hairs on his head to “stop people in their tracks.”

For Varambhia, the contrast was hard to miss. He’s South Asian, with naturally deep-black hair, so the grays stood out. The Journal of Clinical and Aesthetic Dermatology reports that people of Asian descent also tend to go gray later, often in their late 30s. According to Professor Desmond Tobin, a professor of dermatological science at University College Dublin, many Eastern cultures have traditionally been less accepting of grays than the West, with men and women both more likely to dye them.

“At first, I felt like I didn’t want to seem a lot older than my peers,” Varambhia says. Over time, though, he came to see the silver as something that set him apart. “I’ve always been one to stand out, and it was another attributing factor to that.”

When there are too many gray hairs to pluck, do you reach for the Just for Men, or join the silver fox cub club?

Turning gray at any age can make you wonder why it’s happening. According to Tobin, the answer is usually in your DNA. “The first thing to do is just to look at your [previous] generation of relatives,” he says. “They’re going to give you a road map for where you’re going to be. And if you have early grayers—teens, early 20s—then that’s really mapped out quite starkly in the family.”

Other things can speed up the process, too. Stress is a big one. Tobin says it can damage the cells responsible for producing hair pigment, acting like a “sledgehammer” if the process has already begun. Just look at Barack Obama’s hair before and after his time in the White House. Nutrition can play a role, too, and a blood test can pick up any deficiencies. But Tobin says the nutritional deficiencies linked to premature graying tend to show up in countries where malnutrition is more widespread.

Crucially, though, Tobin says he hasn’t seen hard evidence that young people are going gray earlier across the board. Modern life might come with its share of chronic stressors, he points out, but humans have been enduring trying times since long before social media, climate change, and cost of living crises entered the picture.

Tobin is skeptical of the “overstated claims” made by supplements and serums that promise to “tickle your stem cells.” If you want to slow the process, he says, your best bet is the boring stuff: less stress, plenty of sleep and an antioxidant-rich diet. That might hold things off for a while. But for anyone genetically predisposed to graying, it might not make that much of a difference.

“Would maximum antioxidant intake really stop somebody from graying who’s genetically headed that way? It’s not likely to be a very significant contributor,” Tobin says. “It might, in a multi-prong way, add up to something, but if you’re graying significantly in your teens, that is probably the die that’s being cast for you.”

The good news? Going gray early doesn’t mean the rest of you is aging at the same rate. In fact, there may be a silver lining. People who go gray prematurely are more likely to keep their hair; “nearly all people who bald [do so] with their natural hair color,” says Tobin.

But for anyone who doesn’t want to live with their grays, the hunt for a treatment is on. Research into gray hair has surged alongside the wider boom in longevity science. Tobin says the past five years have felt like a return to the 1980s, when the topical hair-regrowth drug Rogaine first shook up the haircare world. He has consulted for startups developing anti-gray treatments aimed at reducing oxidative stress or extending the life of melanocyte stem cells. The science is promising, but so far, nothing has produced evidence strong enough to satisfy regulators.

Researchers are beginning to understand that graying may not be irreversible, as once thought. Melissa Harris at the University of Alabama has studied the melanocyte stem cells behind hair pigmentation, and immunotherapy as a way to reverse graying. Other research has shown that some hairs can spontaneously regain their pigment, with changes appearing to track periods of stress. It hints that some aspects of aging may be less linear than we assume.

Dr. Greg Williams, a hair restoration surgeon at the Farjo Hair Institute, has watched the field develop. He hosts conference sessions on gray hair and, a couple of years ago, set up a WhatsApp group where specialists around the world could swap ideas and compare notes. “Gray hair is very poorly understood,” he says. “There’s not a lot of research in humans around it.” Maybe that’s surprising, given how universal it seems to be. But commercial fallout is huge. The global hair-and scalp-care industry is worth nearly $9.4 billion, and Williams thinks anyone who figures it out “would be a Nobel Prize winner”.

As for Varambhia, the science seems to fit his experience. Genetics are clearly part of the picture: his dad’s hair has been white for years, and he still has a full head of it. Varambhia also suspects stress sped things up. His grays arrived rapidly as he settled into corporate nine-to-five life. Since hitting the gym regularly, he thinks the process may have slowed.

Knowing other people going through the same thing has helped. “I know eventually I’ll go fully gray. I hope it’s a bit later on than sooner,” he says. But when the time comes? “I’ll embrace it.”

A version of this story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of British GQ with the title “Gray Ahead of Schedule”

#6761. Kylie Jenner and Timothée Chalamet Swap Courtside Maximalism for Sushi Casual

Kylie Jenner and Timothée Chalamet made something of an art form out of courtside couple dressing. Between their coordinating looks and unabashed PDA, the pair were as much a spectacle as the triumphant basketball games that played out during the Knicks’s historic run. But for a rare date night in Los Angeles, they traded the satsuma orange and Chrome Hearts theatrics for much more low-key.

The couple were spotted heading into Sushi Park, the famously unassuming West Hollywood restaurant whose second-floor strip-mall walkway has become an unofficial paparazzi runway. Kendall Jenner, Hailey Bieber, and Zoë Kravitz are among the famous faces who have turned the short walk from the parking lot to the restaurant into a street-style moment. Jenner and Chalamet, however, kept things casual. Jenner wore an oversized black leather jacket over a simple white tank top, paired with relaxed blue jeans and Khaite’s minimal Sophie sandals in patent leather. Her long, dark hair fell in loose waves, while her makeup was similarly pared back. Chalamet opted for a black zip-up jacket from Adon (a London-based brand the actor looks to frequently for slick suede and leather bombers, as does Robert Pattinson) layered over a dark T-shirt, loose black suede trousers also from Adon, and a fine gold chain at his neck.

It's a considerable departure from their more exuberant recent public appearances, particularly their courtside outings, where Chalamet embraced his playful and eccentric approach to getting dressed thanks to a close partnership with Chrome Hearts and his enduring penchant for oversized streetwear.

For Jenner, the pared-back ensemble also comes hot on the heels of one of her most theatrical fashion moments yet. Just weeks ago, she made a surprise appearance at the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show, donned the famed Fantasy Bra in a glittering, jewel-encrusted look complete with feathered wings for her official runway debut. Of course, in recent years Jenner has relied on a more laidback day-to-day uniform, whether in her own KHY leather pieces or new denim launches.

But remember: we’re hurtling toward the festive and awards seasons (plus, the Dune: Part Three press tour), where Kylie Jenner and Timothée Chalamet will certainly find their maximalist groove once again.

#6760. Why Do We Keep Coming Back to Godzilla?

Like most people born in the western world after 1954, I knew of a monster named Godzilla before I’d heard of a country called Japan. It’s not a rare perspective. For as long as nearly any living person can remember, Godzilla has been hugely popular, far exceeding the global recognition afforded most leaders, athletes, and certainly his human co-stars, those men and women whose dramatic dealings are the universally agreed-upon bargain for apocalyptic havoc that millions upon millions have gladly paid to see for over 70 years.

What Godzilla hasn’t been is prestigious. The irradiated dinosaur of very few words—mainly deafening screams—is a beloved figure more in the key of Harry Potter, Batman, or James Bond than the loftier fare that typically gets recognized by major festivals or awards bodies. But exceptions to the rule do exist. The Criterion Collection issued Ishiro Honda's 1954 original on a deluxe Blu-ray in 2012 and marked their 1,000th release with a lavish set of Godzilla’s Shōwa era (1954-1975), which both documents film history and calls to mind John Huston’s Chinatown line about sticking around long enough to become respectable. Writing an introduction to Honda’s biography A Life in Film, from Godzilla to Kurosawa, Martin Scorsese deemed the series’ first director "an extraordinary artist and craftsman" and placed Honda’s 1954 film (alongside spin-offs Mothra and Rodan) among “pictures that haunted the imaginations of young moviegoers like myself and millions of others for years to come.” Kiyoshi Kurosawa—perhaps Japan’s greatest living filmmaker, and certainly its finest practitioner of austere horror—deemed Honda’s film an early lesson in national trauma. Thomas Pynchon, too, had ambitious plans to write a novel about the insurance adjuster who responds to Godzilla’s wreckage (the idea never fully materialized, though it did become a nice tangent in his 1990 novel Vineland). We can look at the films and countenance such appreciations from geniuses. In the meantime, good luck to anyone counting the exact number of action figures, coloring books, sticker packs, and lunchboxes boasting Godzilla and friends.

A critical shift started somewhere in the last decade. Toho ostensibly shelved Godzilla with 2004’s Final Wars, a grand finale that was the big-budget equivalent of a child smashing toys. (That is a compliment.) Godzilla LLC boasts enough films and ancillary products to be financially solvent until the sun burns out (or an indomitable monster rises from the sea), but that retirement was short-lived. Toho resurrected the franchise in aggressive form with 2016’s Shin Godzilla, both a complete series reboot and (across several stages, each grotesque and terrifying) character reinvention that dispensed with typical monster-movie hijinks. An unholy experiment that would make the series’ many mad scientists proud, Shin Godzilla fused the hilarious bureaucratic hallway-rushing of In the Loop or Veep with cosmic horror befitting its director, Hideaki Anno, whose contributions to Japanese culture are nearly Godzilla’s equal on the strength of Neon Genesis Evangelion alone. With time, it’s downright eerie to see Anno’s foreshadowing of the global unrest of Trump, Brexit, COVID, and our endless misinformation era. But rather than keep the great filmmaker on retainer for any number of sequels or spin-offs, Toho regrettably followed Shin Godzilla with an anime trilogy produced for Netflix, a rare class of films I’ve been too embarrassed to even log on Letterboxd.

Whether genius or dreck, none of these films quite anticipated 2023’s Godzilla Minus One. It’s one thing to become the highest-grossing Japanese-language release in U.S. history—any Godzilla movie that doesn't at least aim for such a distinction isn’t worth its salt. It’s harder to imagine anyone, even or especially the franchise’s most ardent fans, anticipated lavish public praise from a quintet of Best Director winners in Steven Spielberg (who claims to have seen the film at least three times), James Cameron (who offered to work on a sequel), Christopher Nolan, Guillermo Del Toro, and Bong Joon-ho. In a recorded conversation with Yamazaki, Tom Cruise and Christopher McQuarrie credited the film as an inspiration; the actor went so far as to call it “perfect.” Which is to say nothing of the fact that Godzilla Minus One earned the series its first Oscar, a VFX prize awarded to none other than writer-director Takashi Yamazaki—a nice encapsulation of the film’s balance between spirit and spectacle.

Because Godzilla Minus One resonated more than anyone could have anticipated, Godzilla Minus Zero, which arrives in theaters this weekend, shoulders the burden of expectation. The first shot across the bow was its world premiere at last month’s New York Film Festival, where Zero debuted alongside prestige fare like Luca Guadagnino’s Sam Altman biopic Artificial and Tony Gilroy’s star-studded Behemoth! There, NYFF’s artistic director Dennis Lim introduced the movie to a sold-out crowd at the Alice Tully Hall, praising Yamazaki for exemplifying "the endless possibilities of creative VFX and genre filmmaking." Minus Zero indeed thrives on spectacle: its opening sequence blazes a merciless path of carnage aided by an elegant long take, while subsequent scenes centered on one of Godzilla’s classic foes, the three-headed Ghidorah, inspire genuine terror more than cheap thrills. This is the first Japanese film shot for IMAX, a large-scale exhibition that’s often torqued for discomfort. Yamazaki’s centerpiece battle pitting the monster against a squadron of fighter planes is dogged by fatalistic doom: a mother and child screaming as their house crashes to the ground don’t get the last-minute salvation we’ve come to expect, and even the sentimental-seeming inclusion of a pilot named Honda is complicated by his eventual fate.

If not quite so grave as the visions of death that imprinted upon a young Kiyoshi Kurosawa, Yamazaki’s narrative—including a face-melting sequence that U.S. distributor GKIDS have asked not be spoiled—provides a stark contrast to the bloodless carnage of American blockbusters. It’s quite the cultural coup, too, that a film this brutal and generally skeptical of American imperialism has secured a simultaneous international release: every English-speaking actor (none recognizable, each portraying arrogant allied military) bears an amusingly off-market quality à la the nefarious white time-travelers of 1991’s altogether underrated Godzilla vs. King Ghidorah.

Nearly all Godzilla films are a total restart. Minus Zero is immediately notable as a direct sequel. The results suggest that Yamazaki overestimated his initial project. Godzilla is not much of a presence in Minus Zero, first appearing around the 50-minute mark and then again in a suitable, albeit brief climax. Far more time is spent with those we came to know and like during Minus One, each reprised by an entirely competent ensemble that fails to elevate interest higher than a certain co-star’s 50 meters. (The protagonists’ adopted daughter from Minus One returns here for the sole purpose of standing in place and crying.) Whatever its pretensions towards grandeur, this is still a film where a scientist explains his master plan by smashing together two monster figures. It takes little to delight in this moment, a literal and figurative collision of serious storytelling and childlike abandon. More of that spirit might’ve served the final picture.

My mild disappointment is nothing compared to the agony Godzilla has wreaked on the district of Ginza, which last week hosted the first public screenings of Minus Zero through the Tokyo International Film Festival. Godzilla’s annihilated the city’s landmarks enough times that when we think of all-out destruction on the sprawling megalopolis that is Tokyo, we’re probably picturing its uber-fashionable shopping sector laid to ruins. When he emerged from the sea in 1954, he wrecked the district’s historic Nichigeki Theater, which was again destroyed (in real life, of humans’ accord) and reopened as the Yurakucho Mullion building in 1984—the same year as The Return of Godzilla, where he inflicted yet more damage on the brand-new structure. Godzilla Minus One recreated the Nichigeki Theater in loving detail only to destroy it again in a sequence that deployed modern visual effects to cause maximum tactility and human misery. That Hiroshima-like site is revisited in Minus Zero, leading one character to declare that “Ginza is again the battleground.” Time’s healed those wounds, however. The night before Minus Zero’s Japanese premiere, a small crowd gathered outside the Yurakucho Mullion’s Seiko watch for an extravagant display: the clock lifted, and out came a small set of Godzilla puppets that strike chimes while Akira Ifukube’s iconic theme plays.

Ginza’s Wako Clock Tower has fared no better: it was torn to ribbons in the 1954 original, 1995’s Godzilla vs. Destoroyah, Shin Godzilla, and Minus One. After a time you must wonder what keeps bringing Godzilla back to the neighborhood. Is it animal instinct, or mere coincidence, or an atomic hatred for its glowing consumerism? The Wako Annex, a nearby pastry shop that Google not-inaccurately describes as “chic,” currently advertises a Christmas-themed cake recreation of the oft-doomed Clock Tower with Minus Zero’s monster looming over it; accompanying text informs us that “Godzilla strikes Ginza’s Wako once again.” The nearby Toho cinema is within Hibiya Godzilla Square, featuring a small but impressively detailed statue and a plaque reading, “The human race must coexist with Godzilla.” Immediately adjacent is a three-story shopping mall hosting a Toho store selling numerous Godzilla ephemera. (I bought the tea cup.)

Even Americans accustomed to the fetishization of disaster would be impressed by Japan’s ability to marry suffering and spectacle to great financial gain. Perhaps it’s the same haunted fascination of a young Martin Scorsese or Kiyoshi Kurosawa cast unto the world at large; Godzilla might be scary as he destroys Ginza, but he’s something to admire when shrunk down to human size and displayed inside the Yurakucho Mullion’s lobby. (The photo-snapping crowds that gather every time I walk past suggest that bit of marketing paid off.) Even as Minus Zero’s first reviews show a less rapturous response than Minus One, this series’ viability—and its newfound prestige—shows little indication of flagging. Ginza’s residents were beyond happy to see Godzilla, at least in ossified, action-figure form. But who's to say they wouldn't be just as excited if, one day, his path of destruction broke through the screen?

#6758. A Definitive Guide to the Best Black Boots

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Out of all the fall fashion purchases you make this season, there’s one item that is guaranteed to give you maximum mileage in the months ahead: a pair of black boots.

For those who favor a sleek silhouette to elevate and anchor your outfits, a classic knee-high boot complements dresses and skirts across the board. If you’d like your boots to serve as more of a personality piece, why not test drive a Western-inspired pair, or one detailed with moto hardware? Of course, timeless shapes like square-toe booties and Chelsea boots will always be reliable go-tos; trendier styles like the slouchy boot still feel just as versatile as they did decades ago.

Ahead, we break down nine different black boot styles, from equestrian-inspired riding boots to all-weather lug sole boots. Find the right one for you in our guide to the best black boots of the season.

A quintessential knee-high black boot can be worn layered beneath dresses and skirts or styled over slim trousers and denim. Let your personal preference inform the height—flat or block heels are best for commuters, while a higher heel can transition from the workday to evening seamlessly.

If you’re not yet a Western boot convert, the easiest way to style these silhouettes is to contrast them with wardrobe staples that don’t feel cowboy adjacent. Whether you’re letting your boots peek out from beneath a pair of wool trousers, or opting for a pair with subtler motifs, there’s always a way to make them feel your own.

Saint Laurent Dylan 45 Patent Leather Boots

A traditional Chelsea boot silhouette is the workhorse of many wardrobes. While a classic take on this style makes a great investment, don’t miss modern interpretations, like the Row’s, which are cut extra low.

Manolo Blahnik Chelsa Leather Chelsea Ankle Booties

Whether you’re going for moto-inspired hardware or something with a bit of downtown edge (Coach and Vince, as examples), a silver buckle is the kind of detail that adds dimension while staying versatile.

Manolo Blahnik Calica Buckled Biker Boots

For instant polish, slip into a pair of black riding boots. This equestrian-inspired silhouette is both classic and distinct, ideal with preppy separates like pleated skirts and boxy blazers.

The Row Nobilis Leather Knee-High Riding Boots

Khaite, Chloé, and several other designers included the slouchy boot in their fall 2026 collections, highlighting how this style has shifted away from its ’80s associations and in a much more compelling direction (particularly when rendered in classic black).

Preparing for the winter means being ready for the storms ahead, and a pair of lug-sole boots is a must. The season’s best styles offer all-weather protection (and are a stylish snow-boot alternative), while still feeling versatile enough to wear on glorious days when the weather is agreeable.

Not quite an ankle boot or a knee-high either—the mid-calf boot is shaping up to be one of the season’s most interesting shapes. Need proof? Just look to the designers embracing it—from cool-girl favorite Maryam Nassir Zadeh to buzzy newcomer Jude.

There’s a time and place for a pointed boot, but a sleek square toe always manages to feel a touch cooler. Look for styles with a slimmer, subtler square toe, so you can style them for any occasion.

#6756. The Best Korean Exfoliator to Get (and Maintain) Glass Skin

If there’s one common denominator among Korean skin-care products, be they cleansers or toners, it’s that they prioritize the skin barrier. With this context, the best Korean exfoliators may seem a little counterintuitive—after all, face exfoliants can sometimes do more harm than good, relying on strong active ingredients (or high concentrations) and inadvertently leaving skin dry or irritated.

But that’s the beauty of the Korean approach to resurfacing skin. “K-beauty favors gentle, more frequent exfoliation that protects the skin barrier,” says Jane Yoo, MD, a board-certified dermatologist in New York, NY. “To this effect, K-beauty utilizes low-concentration acids, enzyme powders, or peeling gels that roll dead skin off the surface.” These light-handed exfoliating formulas also considered a critical part of a daily routine aimed towards getting (and maintaining) glass skin—and a natural counterpart to moisturizing. “Exfoliating is usually followed by layers of hydration to support the barrier as the goal is the same: achieve smooth skin but with less irritation,” says Dr. Yoo. With that in mind, consider these worth adding to your regimen.

Exfoliation works on multiple levels to refine and resurface skin—making it one of the most impactful steps in any skin-care routine. At its most basic, “exfoliation helps to remove the outermost layer of dead skin cells in the stratum corneum, promoting skin cell turnover and revealing brighter, smoother and more even-toned skin,” says Dr. Chang.

It can also clear debris from pores, which helps reduce whiteheads, blackheads, and other forms of acne. And in helping shed those dead skin cells that would otherwise pile up on skin’s surface, “exfoliation can also help improve the absorption of active ingredients like vitamin C or retinoids,” she says.

Unlike Western exfoliation habits—which tend to be more aggressive—“Korean skin-care routines emphasize the regular use of gentler topicals to achieve radiant skin without irritation,” says Dr. Chang. One big point of difference is that in the U.S., many formulations tend to focus on correcting issues, be they acne, dullness, or dark spots.

However, Koreans prioritize prevention and consistency. As a result, “you’ll rarely find harsh scrubs or aggressive peels,” says Cho. “The majority of K-beauty exfoliants use mild chemical exfoliants such as PHA, because they clear dead skin cells without irritation.” Fermented enzymes and natural fruit extracts are also popular, since they tend to be well tolerated even by those with sensitive skin, according to Dr. Chang.

How often you exfoliate ultimately depends on your skin type and the sort of exfoliant you’re using. For example, if you have dry or sensitive skin, exfoliating once or twice a week with a mild acid may suffice, says Dr. Chang. Meanwhile, “for those with oily or acne-prone skin, people may tolerate light daily exfoliation with ingredients like salicylic acid,” she says.

Ultimately, the strength of your exfoliant plays a large role. “Higher strength exfoliants should be used less frequently, while very gentle exfoliators could be used daily in those who tolerate it well,” says Dr. Chang. If you’re using a Korean exfoliant, then it’s likely gentle enough to use daily or every other day (but again, that depends). Ultimately, she says, “the key is to monitor for signs of over-exfoliation—like stinging, redness, flaking, or tightness—and adjust the frequency accordingly.”

When we test and review a product, we take a holistic approach to deliver well-rounded product recommendations. First, we lean on Vogue’s vast network of experts—from board-certified dermatologists to celebrity estheticians—to gain professional acumen on the industry’s standout products, ones these specialists would actually use on their clients. We pair their expertise with our editorial best practices to curate the thoughtful edits you read on our site.

As it relates to the best Korean exfoliator, we selected the best based on the following characteristics: ingredients, texture, exfoliant type, and the skin-care concerns they address. To do this, we paired our own personal tests of each formula with expert guidance and reviewer insights to determine which we would recommend to you.

The internet has some peculiar ideas about what constitutes a good gift for a boyfriend. Surely the person you love deserves something a little more inspired.

#6752. In Brooklyn, an Intimate Diwali Dinner Party Bridging Two Easts

On a Sunday night, the elevator doors open onto Rachel Gurjar’s Brooklyn living room. Amaranthus blooms brush brass family heirlooms and fresh pomegranates on a credenza. A tiffin bearing a relative’s initials rests on draped brown fabric. The room could be a still from Gurjar’s debut cookbook, East to East: Recipes and Stories From Mumbai to New York, released October 13.

Barefoot in a pre-draped blue floral sari, Gurjar moves unhurriedly, labelling plates with scraps of tape. She then pulls a white platter from an immaculately organized glass cabinet. “I think this is for the duck,” she says, already moving on.

Since 2021, Gurjar, who grew up in Mumbai, has thrown New York Diwali parties that have acquired a certain lore. One year, she led magazine editors in choreographed Bollywood dances; another, a pani puri fountain presided over a party for 140, with handmade naan to match. “I didn’t have a place to celebrate Diwali myself, so I decided to throw these parties,” she says.

Tonight, the sixth annual gathering is quieter, and intentionally so: a dozen chefs, artists, and designers who helped bring the book to life, on the page or in spirit. If the official launch party was for the public, this one is for her people.

Diwali, the South Asian festival of lights, is widely associated with the triumph of good over evil. It is also an occasion to gather, feed one another, and take stock. Gurjar’s chosen family spans disciplines, with an emphasis on the Black, Brown, and AAPI creatives whose work, she believes, makes New York what it is. “I wanted to make sure that every person at the table had a medium through which they expressed their creativity,” she explains.

Guests include collaborators like designer Ivan Lam of Tender Touch Studios, who picked up flowers that morning; food stylist Sean Dooley, who styled the book’s visuals; and photographer Isa Zapata, a former Bon Appétit colleague who photographed it in Mumbai.

The evening begins with an actually spicy pani puri mezcalita and masala popcorn. Whipped masala and curry leaf butters sit in vintage coupes beside a sweet-sour pomegranate Chex Mix that took Gurjar 12 attempts to perfect. Bollywood music plays as guests pass around bindis and apply them to one another.

The menu moves between Mumbai and New York, yet feels unmistakably familial. Gurjar’s mother, Ranjana, brought potato and rice chips she made in India, sneaking them past TSA. She also spent the week helping her daughter prepare, kneading dough and frying gulab jamun.

“At her core, Rachel is a nurturer,” says chef Josh Osamor, who calls her goat curry one of the most delicious things he has eaten. The two recently collaborated on a Nigerian-Indian supper club. “The biryani jollof was a no-brainer. It was a perfect dance,” he says.

Leanne Gan, an art director at the ACLU, has attended Gurjar’s Diwali parties since the beginning. Adjusting her sea-green dupatta, she dips a rice chip into the Penang curry crab dip. “Rachel is someone who doesn’t settle for less,” Gan says. “It’s been amazing to see her put 110% of herself into these recipes.”

The room erupts with oohs and aahs as artist and fashion designer Misha Japanwala arrives in a gold-and-silver lamé sari and a custom silver breastplate she made using body casting, the same process she used for pieces for Cardi B and Lizzo. (Most recently, Lupita Nyong’o wore Japanwala’s work in The Odyssey.)

“I just felt like I was missing a silver breastplate in my wardrobe, and everybody needs a silver breastplate,” she jokes. She accessorizes with nipple-cast rings and silver jewelry from Karachi, her hometown.

“One of the most beautiful things about moving to New York has been expanding my South Asian circle and being able to celebrate each other in this way,” Japanwala reflects. When she hosted Eid at home for the first time last year, she invited friends from her wider South Asian circle, beyond Muslim Pakistanis. “It really was the most miraculous thing,” she adds. Next to her, fashion designer Sheena Sood of Abacaxi is wearing a mirrorwork lehenga with a matching knit shrug from a 2021 collection.

The contagious joys of Diwali extend beyond the subcontinent: For Manouska Jeantus, founder of the social events company Nous Love Club, the gathering recalls celebrating Haitian Independence Day with her family, even as she eats malai shrimp instead of the soup joumou she grew up with.

Guests take their seats, and Gurjar raises a dirty chai espresso martini for a quick toast. Dinner is a greatest-hits collection from East to East, starting with achari melon salad with tempered pickling spices and garam masala roasted squash slicked with brown butter and lime. Back in the kitchen, Gurjar works two cast-iron skillets at once, searing scored duck breasts for her paanch phoran duck—a crowd favorite—and finishing them with raspberry chimichurri. Platters of rice arrive with naan she and her mother made that morning.

Dessert brings homemade gulab jamun and cardamom sticky toffee cake, which Gurjar floods with caramel and finishes with flaky salt. Osamor shakes up another round of dirty chai espresso martinis. A little dancing gives way to conversation on the couch about the week ahead. It is, after all, a school night, and everyone is pleasantly overfed.

The meal mirrors the book’s central idea: a refusal to draw a neat line between East and West. East to East also makes room for what a cooking reel can leave out: the distance from home, building a career abroad, and watching parents grow older from afar, and other complexities that extend beyond homesickness.

In a rare chapter titled “Cultural Appropriation: Who Really Pays,” Gurjar asks who really benefits when food travels, and who gets left behind. It’s a question she and her creative peers have long discussed. To her, celebrating food’s ability to connect people also means asking who gets to tell its stories.

When I first met Gurjar two years ago at a party, I told her she’d taught me to make perfect short ribs, her Thai-inflected coconut braise. What I didn’t tell her was how rare it felt to see another Indian immigrant pursue a creative path, or how brave I thought she was. She has become that person for others, too, inspiring adventurous cooking and a willingness to take up space.

Some had made a career like hers seem out of reach. “I had experiences where people made me feel it wasn’t an option for me,” Gurjar laments. “Brown people, we’re often scared to dream big because we’ve never been permitted to. And I’m in a phase of life when I’m not saying no to anything.”

Asked about the highlight of her career, Gurjar doesn’t have to look backward. She sips her chai espresso martini and says, “I think I’m living it right now.”

#6751. Montana’s Newest Congressman Dresses Like a Ralph Lauren Model

You couldn’t ask for a better embodiment of the blue wave than Sam Forstag, the 32-year-old rookie Montana Democrat who defeated his Republican rival in yesterday’s midterms to flip an important House seat. While unaffordability was a key issue in the midterms across the country, it’s especially noticeable in Montana, where an influx of billionaires and tech companies has sent the cost of living soaring. Like many of his compatriots, Forstag campaigned on promises of affordable housing, better healthcare, and getting money out of politics. Unlike his fellow Democratic challengers, however, he did it in a wardrobe that looked straight off a rack at the RRL store.

Last night, in front of a crowd of supporters in Missoula, Forstag delivered his victory speech dressed in a hickory-striped work shirt, jeans, a big western belt buckle, and a pair of weathered brown forestry boots. Forstag’s fit stands in contrast to the standard election night business-casual look favored by political hopefuls on both sides of the aisle, but he’s far from the first candidate to lean into the dress codes of blue-collar Americans. From Tim Walz’s camo hats and Filson Mackinaw jackets to John Fetterman’s Carhartt hoodie, workwear remains a potent symbol of working-class solidarity. In this case, however, it’s not just blue-collar cosplay.

Forstag at a student get-out-the-vote gathering at Montana State University in October.

Straight out of college and looking to pay off his student loans, Forstag joined the US Forest Service as a wildland firefighter and spent the next eight years battling blazes around the country, including four years as a smokejumper parachuting into fire zones. As a result, he comes by his love of Levi’s and sturdy, Goodyear-welted footwear honestly, and—Montana being a place where jeans and work shirts are considered business-casual for many—didn’t need to change his look for the campaign.

Forstag addressing voters at a town hall in August.

As authentic as Forstag’s election-night outfit was, he also appears to be a guy who understands the power of styling. While his shirt likely didn’t have Ralph Lauren’s initials on the tag (wildland firefighters only make $20 an hour), the way he wore it—half unbuttoned, layered over a red thermal henley, and tucked into his jeans—owes a debt to the romantic take on Western style Lauren has been trafficking in since the ’70s. Or, equally likely, the Montana cowboys who the designer borrowed it from. Either way, Forstag’s look, like the progressive politics he and his Democratic colleagues campaigned on, will be a welcome disruption to business as usual on Capitol Hill.

#6749. Coty Sales Decline 2% as Transition Kicks In

For Coty’s first quarter 2027 earnings, the 2% like-for-like sales decline shows an improvement on the last year, when revenues fell 6% and 4% in the first and second halves of fiscal 2026, respectively. The stabilization — sales were at -1% in Q4 2026 — can be credited to growth in key markets like the US, Asia-Pacific and Latin America, as well as fruitful reinvestment in the group’s prestige business that includes Burberry, Hugo Boss and Calvin Klein.

Still, executive chair and interim CEO Markus Strobel was careful to refer to the progress as “green shoots”, which he hopes will translate into positive growth over time. “As we’ve previously shared, 2027 will be a transition year,” he said in a statement released on Wednesday. “We expect the recovery to be uneven, and implementation will take time. We know confidence is earned through consistent delivery, and our goal is to steadily improve execution and translate it into shareholder value in the years ahead.”

Coty.Curated, the French beauty group’s turnaround framework, kicked into motion last fiscal year. A redesign of the organization and cost structure hopes to improve conversion through reinvestment into its portfolio of brands.

The strategy is paying off the most in the prestige segment. Net revenues declined 1% to $1.1 billion in Q1, a contrast to the same quarter a year prior (-4%), and representing 68% of total sales. “We have increasingly concentrated investment behind our top six prestige brands — Burberry, Hugo Boss, Calvin Klein, Marc Jacobs, Chloé, and Kylie Cosmetics — which represented 73% of prestige sales in Q1,” Strobel noted, highlighting the success of new launches including Boss Bottled Beyond for Her, Burberry Goddess Amber Vanilla, Mood Stones by Kylie Jenner, and makeup from Marc Jacobs Beauty.

Earlier this week, Coty announced a 40-year extension of its Marc Jacobs license, which it has held since 2003. The plan is to expand the brand across multiple categories following the successful relaunch of Marc Jacobs Beauty in May. (The beauty offshoot was discontinued in 2021, once its licensing deal with LVMH-owned Kendo ended.)

Strobel said: “The recent launch of Marc Jacobs Beauty makeup is off to a strong start across the US, the UK, Canada, and Australia. The agreement provides Coty with a strong platform to build on that momentum while enhancing the long-term visibility of Coty’s Prestige portfolio.”

The recovery of consumer beauty — including CoverGirl, Rimmel, and Sally Hansen — is slightly slower. While the decline in like-for-like sales narrowed to 3% from 8% in the first half of fiscal 2026, the segment remains negative. Net revenues reached $496.8 million in Q1, declining 3% on a like-for-like basis.

Asia-Pacific led growth across both segments, with sales up 8% to $192 million, driven by higher sales in China, Southeast Asia, and Asia travel retail. Sales were flat across the Americas at $653 million, with particular momentum in Brazil. EMEA (Europe, the Middle East, and Africa) revenues fell 5% to $710 million; the group cited lower sales in the Middle East and Europe.

Coty expects to see a low single-digit decline in Q2, consistent with the last two quarters. As for the full year, the company is still withholding its projection as it gears up to claw back the negative growth seen throughout 2026.

#6747. Abdul El-Sayed Is Winning at Watches, Too

Michigan voters made history Tuesday night by electing Abdul El-Sayed to the US Senate, as part of a greater blue wave that swept the nation. The 44-year-old former epidemiologist is set to become America’s first Muslim senator—and, in a slightly more trivial milestone, might also be the first real-deal watch nerd to reach the Capitol.

During his victory speech, the senator-elect sported a Grand Seiko Spring Drive Chronograph (ref. SBGC249), a limited-edition piece from the Japanese watchmaker that pairs a GMT (which tracks the time across multiple time zones) and a chronograph (i.e. a stopwatch function). Politicians tend to go one of two ways with their timepieces: Either they’ll cosplay as a man of the people with an affordable Timex or Casio, or they’ll sport a luxury watch with historical pedigree—like the Rolex “President” Day-Date, worn by several commanders-in-chief—to make themselves appear ready for higher office. An obscure Grand Seiko fits neither of those categories.

Instead, El-Sayed’s Grand Seiko is a burly sport model made by one of the world’s most elegant watchmakers. At 43.5mm, it’s hefty, a good fit for the senator-elect’s beefed-up forearms. It’s quite complicated, with several different functions, and a dial as busy as Little Caesars Arena during a Detroit Pistons game. It costs $12,400, but you’d have to be very deep into the hobby to recognize its value.

What I find so compelling about El-Sayed’s choice is that it doesn’t appear to be masquerading as some political symbol. It’s simply the excellent wristwear of an honest-to-goodness collector. “Folks who know me know I’m a sucker for automatic watches and sort of classic watches,” El-Sayed said in an interview with LGBT Detroit in May.

Throughout his campaign, he’s worn a wide range of pieces. His recent go-to appears to be a Mühle-Glashütte S.A.R. Rescue-Timer, a great-looking and obscure German tool watch. He also owns the U-1 SE, a large dive watch from the German independent brand Sinn.

Not all of El-Sayed’s timepieces are devoid of political meaning, however. The senator-elect has worn an Omega Speedmaster, the watch worn by American astronauts Buzz Aldrin and Neil Armstrong when they first set foot on the moon. That’s about as patriotic as a watch can get without excerpting the Declaration of Independence on the dial. Naturally, he also owns a Shinola Runwell, from the brand based in El-Sayed’s native Detroit. El-Sayed is very aware of what a certain watch can signal about the wearer. In his 2020 book, Healing Politics: A Doctor’s Journey into the Heart of Our Political Epidemic, he wrote about how seeing Rolex billboards along a highway prompted him to reflect on income inequality: “How many people who drive this road can actually afford one of those?”

But when did I realize that El-Sayed was a true-blue watch guy? It wasn’t the dialed-in collection of interesting tool watches that did it, but his appearance at a rally on Sunday. He wasn’t wearing a timepiece at all, surprisingly. Which meant that there, on his wrist, was the mark of a true collector: a watch tan.

#6746. No-Knead Pumpkin Focaccia

For those who fall into the pumpkin-should-be-savory camp, let us offer up a new recipe primed to become a seasonal staple: Pumpkin Focaccia. The iconic bread we know and love is ripe for the pumpkin treatment, lending it a golden hue and earthy sweetness. This version doesn’t require any kneading, making it straightforward enough to pull off in a single afternoon. You’ll perform a series of folds as it rises, simply pulling and stretching the dough to help it develop gluten. A showering of fresh rosemary and flaky salt on top is optional, but it takes crisp, plush focaccia from good to great. Serve it with a bowl of olive oil for dipping or a schmear of cream cheese to lean into the seasonal profile.

This recipe is part of Pumpkin Week 2026, Bon Appétit’s annual celebration of autumn’s most magical pantry staple.

Mix one ¼-oz. envelope active dry yeast (about 2¼ tsp.), 5 cups (625 g) all-purpose flour, 1 cup canned pumpkin purée, 1 Tbsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¾ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 1 Tbsp. light brown sugar, and 1½ cups warm water in a large bowl until a dry, shaggy dough forms. Cover with plastic wrap and let sit in a warm spot 15 minutes.

Place a small bowl of room-temperature water on work surface for easy reaching. Uncover dough and moisten hands in water, shaking off excess. Stretch 1 side of dough up and over to meet opposite side; turn bowl 90°. Repeat process 3 more times, turning bowl after each fold. (This constitutes 1 full fold.) If dough doesn’t always stretch to meet opposite side, just aim for the middle. Remoisten your hands and shake off excess water any time dough begins to feel sticky. Cover dough and let sit 30 minutes (set a timer). Repeat entire process 2 more times (folding and resting), about 1 hour more. By the last full fold, the dough should be significantly smoother and tighter.

Pour ¼ cup extra-virgin olive oil into a 13x9" metal baking pan. Lightly oil your hands and punch down dough, then transfer to prepared pan. Sprinkle with flaky sea salt. Turn dough salted side down to coat both sides in oil. Press into an even layer with your fingers. Cover with plastic wrap and let sit in a warm spot until puffy, about 45 minutes.

Place a rack in bottom third of oven; preheat to 450°. Unwrap focaccia and scatter rosemary leaves and more sea salt over if desired; drizzle with oil. Oil your hands and press fingers into focaccia several times to create dimples across surface, making sure to press fingers all the way down to the bottom of the pan. Bake focaccia, rotating pan front to back halfway through, until deep golden brown, 23–28 minutes. Transfer pan to a wire rack and let focaccia cool slightly in pan. Using a metal spatula, turn out focaccia onto rack and let cool completely.

Do Ahead: Focaccia can be made 1 day ahead. Store tightly wrapped at room temperature.
